# A2.1 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

## Context and Objectives

The Government of Iraq, acting through the Ministry of Water Resources and the Ministry of Environment will be Executing Entities to implement their own kind of climate change adaptation project in the country. The analysis of local meteorological data (Annex A-6) indicates that average temperatures have increased by 0.37°C per decade since 1958, with a projected increase of 2.4°C for the period 2010–2039 under the RCP 4.5 scenario and 3.5°C under the RCP 8.5 scenario). Iraq is already experiencing climate change impacts which exacerbate soil salinity, loss of biodiversity, desertification, and water scarcity. The project will address these impacts by reducing adverse effects on water and agriculture. Water scarcity and suboptimal water quality significantly reduce crop yields and increase food insecurity.

## Key Challenges

Significant reduction in the annual amount of Climate Water Availability (CWA) at a rate between -1% to 20% per decade is projected for the Euphrates and Tigris River basins. This will further exacerbate existing water scarcity, leading to increased competition among users (Sarman et al., 2009). The Euphrates and Tigris River basins are faced with increasing demographic pressures, urbanization, and industrialization, which will increase the demand for water resources. Climate change is also expected to bring about increasing use of water in the upper riparian countries compared by variable rainfall patterns and increased evaporation rates. The project will address these challenges through improved water management.

## Project Components

The project has three components: Component 1 focuses on improving water availability, Component 2 addresses water use efficiency, and Component 3 aims to improve livelihoods of vulnerable households in rural communities in Najaf, Karbala, and Mustansiriyah (Annex A-6). The project is designed to have three components. Component 1 will address the reduction of adverse effects on water availability by increasing water supply, reducing water losses, and increasing water use efficiency.

## Project Impact

The project goal is to enhance climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural households. It is expected that the project will reduce the vulnerability of 205,384 people (of which 196,704 are directly benefited) by improving the water distribution systems, stabilizing and increasing water availability at the farm level; increasing crop water productivity through improved irrigation practices; reducing soil salinity; strengthening local water management institutions and the water distribution regime; reducing the adaptation deficit of farming communities via climate change adaptation strategies. The project will also reduce food insecurity in Iraq and increase the overall social and economic instability resulting from rural to urban migration, which is expected to be reduced by 193,132 people (of which 158,364 are directly benefited). Results of this cross-cutting project include: 1) 107,944 directly and 435,153 indirectly. The results will benefit a total population of women expected to be 591,099 (directly).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Using the Emission Gap report an annual emission avoidance through the infrastructure investments in Component 1 and from the mitigation co-benefits of the CRA capacity development activities of Component 2 of about 67,028 tonnes of CO2 equivalent per year (over a period of 35 years) is expected to be achieved under the baseline scenario (Annex 22). In this regard the project considers GHG reductions through the implementation of Component 1 in the context of the Emission Gap report and the mitigation co-benefits from the CRA capacity development activities of Climate Resilient Agriculture of Component 2 as mitigation co-benefits (-1,312,618 tCO2e over years).

The Economic and Financial analysis (Annexes A and Annex B) of the project shows that it is financially and economically viable. The project has a net present value (NPV) of $504 million at 7% discount rate and internal rate of return (IRR) 21% and agricultural margins, which expect to increase around at IRR: 111.721 % on average in farm models. These results are achieved by the project’s focus on increasing crop intensity, energy efficiency, water use efficiency and climate resilient agricultural practices.

The main sources of benefits also include the increase in crop intensity, and energy and water use efficiency. The project is expected to have a positive impact on the environment through its focus with climate change induced water scarcity. The NPV is required to make of increased costs, reduced profits, climate change adaptation and mitigation measures, and other environmental impacts.

## Paradigm Shift: There are several areas in which the project is expected to stimulate, facilitate and support a paradigm shift from traditional practices towards more sustainable and resilient agricultural systems. These include:

- (i) conversion of the open air canal to piped system;
- (ii) increasing energy security by introducing solar powered pumping stations;
- (iii) modernizing the system of water regulation and use; and
- (iv) participation of women as key agents of change in the farming community.

The project is expected to build a network of Climate Wise Women (CWW) who will serve as role models, agents to impart knowledge and establish a cache of Climate Wise Women (CWW). This network of CWW will help to increase awareness on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures. The project also aims at building the leadership skills and ability to adapt to climate change. Shifting the paradigm from women as victims to women as problem solvers is expected to have a positive impact in changing perceptions and stereotypes regarding women and their role in communities.

## Financial Analysis: The project is expected to create new jobs, markets and business opportunities.

The elaboration of the roadmap for rural solar electrification and the installation of solar panels above canals is in fact expected to increase the number of jobs. The introduction of solar powered pumping stations as a new technology is foreseen to create new business activities and incentivize private sector investment as well. Finally, the project is expected to have a positive impact on the environment through its focus with climate change adaptation.

In addition, the project is expected assist the Government in preparing a road map for rural solar electrification. This is expected to increase energy security by introducing solar powered pumping stations and modernizing the system of water regulation and use.

Additionally, the project is aligned with the GCF Strategic Program 2024-2027 particularly Target 5 & 6, 7, and (to be determined).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Context and Background (approximately 2 pages)

The development challenges encountered in Southern Iraq are closely linked to the Water Energy Food (WEF) Nexus which underlines the relationship between climate induced water scarcity, energy deficits and food security concerns. These challenges are exacerbated by increasing temperatures, reduced surface water and by isolated irrigation infrastructure and practices that are not sustainable or resilient to climate change. The combination of these factors results in a situation where water scarcity, energy deficits and climate change adaptation deficit of farming communities directly impact food production and the supply chain. The challenges related to managing natural resources in Southern Iraq highlight the need for integrated water management and climate change adaptation and mitigation while ultimately improving the environment and the quality of life, community resilience and economic development.

**Current Situation:** Southern Iraq is one of the most vulnerable regions in the world to climate change because of its hydrological limitations. Access to water is a geographical problem. The country is located in the arid zone (21°N-35°N) with an average annual rainfall between 0 and 40 mm, which is less than half of the global average precipitation. The climate is mild and with mild to cool winters and dry, hot/dusky summers. Summer temperatures average above 38°C while winter temperatures are mild with average temperatures in the coldest month (January) around 17°C. Annual rainfall range between 200 and 300 mm. The main rivers of Southern Iraq are the Tigris and Euphrates which flow from Turkey to the Persian Gulf and Najaf which are the three targeted governorates, with adverse effects on water availability leading to reduced farming capacity.

### B.2. National and Local Historical Climate Trends

Analysis of historical data indicates that the climate has already changed and that the rate of change is increasing. The study uses 198 meteorological stations* to analyze recorded rainfall and temperature trends between 1941 and 2009. Regression analysis shows a significant increase in mean annual temperatures, with an average increase of 0.5°C/decade for rainfall and 0.6°C/decade respectively, while no significant historical trend could be detected related to minimum temperatures. The study also shows that the precipitation has decreased by 12% (MM) per decade since 1941 in the main precipitation in the source areas of the two rivers within the riparian countries [Senesi et al., 2005; Saeed, 2002]. FAO analysis (2013) confirms that rainfall has decreased by 8% per decade since 1941. The study also shows that precipitation in the watershed occurs on the mountains from November until March and decreases to a minimum during April and May. Furthermore, Figure 2 shows that most of the precipitation of the watershed occurs on the mountain range from November until March and decreases to a minimum during April and May.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5

## Climate Change Impacts

The adverse impacts of climate change are leading to increased saltinity, loss of biodiversity, decreased agricultural yields and increased desertification (Ogal, 2016). This combination of factors, together with obsolete farming practices, has led to a significant decline in the availability of water resources such as rivers, lakes, groundwater springs and aquifers (GURAN2018 ESCWA et al.2017). The aforementioned impacts have significant implications for food security and economic development.

The impact of climate change on rainfall variability was evaluated in target areas and at a national level there is a significant increase in rainfall variability (Almeida et al., 2016). This has led to an overall increase in variability in Karkala, Najaf and on a national level in the period between 2000 and 2060 in an RCP8.5 Scenario (IPCC AR5). The increased rainfall variability is expected to have significant impacts on water availability and infrastructure on a national and local level; variability in rainfall negatively impacts water availability, and a portion of it is eventually lost through evaporation and runoff. This has significant implications for the irrigation sector as well as other water resources which are essential for the irrigation in Southern Governorates.

Climate variability is one of Iraq's main challenges. The country experienced an increase in temperature during 1950-2000, with a number of months experiencing temperatures above normal and a decrease in precipitation (ESCWA et al., 2017). This has led to increased evaporation rates and reduced soil moisture levels which have had significant impacts on agricultural productivity. The historical trends of warmer temperatures will continue in the future and that precipitation is likely to diminish. ESCWA, et al. (2017) reported that IPCC AR5 findings identified an increase by +2.9°C.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.8.0 PAGE 6

Based on the CORDEX-ENA model, temperatures could increase by +2.5°C to +3°C in winter and +2.75°C to +4°C in summer in 2081–2100 compared to 1986–2005 period (RCP 4.5 scenario) (FAR, 2013; EAGLE, 2017). The average annual precipitation is projected to decrease by ~3.50mm (~40.67mm to ~43.69mm) in 2040-2059 (RCP 5.8, Ensemble), [2085], and decrease by >10% until the end of this century compared with the reference period (1986–2005). The projected increase in temperature and decrease in precipitation in the local meteorological data of the target governorates indicates that annual average and maximum temperatures** and **Potential Evapotranspiration will increase, while annual average minimum temperature and precipitation will decrease in the same period in RCP4.5 (Table 3).

The agricultural sector is in the increasing demand for water. Agriculture is the main source of employment for 30% of the population living in rural areas and overall, for 23% of women. The majority of the income of the households comes from agriculture. Agricultural practices are highly dependent on irrigation: 70% of farmers depend on irrigation for wheat production particularly in the governorates of Al-Arisi, Sissakam, and Nadih Nablah. In addition to climate change, there is a decrease in water availability due to climatic (snow and rainfall) and demographic pressures (73.1 million people in 2050 [Boujelbene, 2017]). There are both climatic and non-climatic factors that contribute to the decline of groundwater resources.

Climate change is among the main drivers contributing to the decline in water discharge in the river basins. This is due to a decrease in precipitation (Table 3) and an increase in evaporation (Al-Arabi, 2018; Al-Arabi et al., 2019). Climate change is also one of the main drivers contributing to the decline in groundwater resources. The average annual rainfall has decreased from 457 mm/year between 1960 and 13-year mean was observed at a 0.05 level of significance, indicating an increased need for irrigation for crop production (Al-Arabi et al., 2018). The groundwater resources in the country are under pressure due to over-extraction and pollution. The water balance is negative in most governorates.

This is based on the fact that by reviewing cropping plans in the agro-climatic zones, reducing conveyance loss and reducing evaporation losses from irrigation canals, it will be possible to save 10% of the total water used for agriculture (Al-Arabi et al., 2018). The groundwater resources are under pressure due to over-extraction and pollution. The water balance is negative in most governorates.

The current situation of groundwater use in the country is as follows: In general, groundwater does not satisfy the standards of drinking water except in northern Iraq where it is used for irrigation purposes (Al-Arabi et al., 2018). Groundwater resources are under pressure due to over-extraction and pollution. The water balance is negative in most governorates.

## B (2) | Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

**Iraq's agriculture faces constraints in terms of water resource availability which are expected to worsen over time. These constraints stem from the lack of agreement about water share with upper riparian states, regulatory policy that prescribes its use, and climate change. The first constraint is due to a lack of agreement between Iraq and its upper riparian states; water loss is due to global climate change and on the other it is due to over-use by upper riparian countries which have been releasing large amounts of water into the Tigris River. This has led to increased water losses in the distribution networks, evaporation of water by inefficient irrigation systems, deterioration of water quality from return flows, and reduced water availability for agriculture. The second constraint is due to climate change, which will lead to increased water use and to optimize water use in agriculture by improving agricultural productivity and emphasizing irrigation system efficiency, as well

As working with farmers and extension services to increase the adoption of advanced production technologies and practices investing in policy and legal reforms, and strengthening water resources planning, management, and governance can result in significant benefits for climate resilience. This is particularly true when these efforts are targeted at vulnerable communities on climate-sensitive livelihoods with limited scope for diversification. There is high dependence on crop varieties that are not stress-tolerant, such as rice, which is a major staple food in many countries. In the case of rice and corn farmers are forced to assess alternate options as the state has started to ban the production of rice and corn in years when water availability is low. Farmers have limited access to information on climate change and are not aware of the need for resilient technologies and practices and limited access to actionable climate information. Flood irrigation is still practiced leading to overuse of water resources, which is a major concern as it leads to soil salinity and poor quality of water. Farmers lack access for technology transfer, particularly for small producers, are weak in and need of new climate adaptive skills. Farmers do not have the capacity to adapt their farming practices to changing climatic conditions. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

The inadequate national strategic framework related to water management are the main bottlenecks to climate change adaptation in Iraq and in particular to the farming communities of the central and southern plains. Lack of investment in modern irrigation and drainage systems, coupled with poor maintenance of existing infrastructure, results in inefficient use of water resources and energy to deploy more efficient water use and low carbon emission practices. Furthermore, outdated practices and technologies are still widely used in agriculture, leading to high levels of water loss and inefficiency. The project aims to respond to the following main barriers to adaptation and mitigation: (i) Lack of investment in modern irrigation and drainage systems; (ii) Inadequate national strategic framework related to water management; (iii) High climate change adaptation deficit; (iv) Women's lack of access to land, credit and other financial services; (v) Lack of awareness among farmers on the importance of efficient use of water resources; (vi) Lack of policy for efficient water regulation and use; and (vii) Lack of appropriate incentives for more efficient low emission energy supply systems.

2. Energy in particular is reported as one of the main obstacles to water use efficiency and sustainable management of water resources, especially during summer months when demand for irrigation water supply overlaps with the peak general electric consumption, especially in summer. The main pumping stations used standard diesel engines which are inefficient and polluting. In addition, there is a lack of investment in infrastructure; the energy situation is expected to worsen, given that electricity consumption is projected to double by 2015 compared to current levels. This will further exacerbate the problem of water supply for irrigation purposes. The Ministry of Water Resources has submitted an application for Renewable Energy was submitted to the Ministerial Energy Council and is still pending approval. The law aims to encourage investment in renewable energy projects, including solar power plants, wind farms, biomass, small hydroelectric schemes, and geothermal energy.

3. Barriers to adaptation and mitigation are also related to lack of awareness among farmers on the importance of efficient use of water resources and low carbon emission practices. This is currently not allowed under the current land tenure system in Iraq which does not allow women to contribute to the decision-making process regarding land allocation, especially for irrigation purposes (Oman, 2013, par. 563). The lack of policy on efficient water regulation and use is also a major barrier to adaptation and mitigation.

4. Barriers to adaptation and mitigation are also related to lack of awareness among farmers on the importance of efficient use of water resources and low carbon emission practices. This is currently not allowed under the current land tenure system in Iraq which does not allow women to contribute to the decision-making process regarding land allocation, especially for irrigation purposes (Oman, 2013, par. 563). The lack of policy on efficient water regulation and use is also a major barrier to adaptation and mitigation.

### Main Barriers
- High climate change adaptation deficit and limited access to climate information and services.
- Lack of awareness among farmers on the importance of efficient use of water resources and low carbon emission practices.

### Underlying Factors
- Inadequate national strategic framework related to water management.
- Lack of investment in modern irrigation and drainage systems, coupled with poor maintenance of existing infrastructure.
- Outdated practices and technologies are still widely used in agriculture, leading to high levels of water loss and inefficiency.

### Key Stakeholders
- All Farmers' Movement
- Economists
- Universities/Technical Schools
- NGOs
- CWA
- Ministry of Water Resources
- Ministry of Agriculture
- Ministry of Electricity
- Ministry of Environment
- Ministry of Planning
- Private sector

### Strategies proposed by the project
1. Develop and implement capacity building activities for women's empowerment in the context of the Future of Women in Iraq project in fields normally dominated by male representation.
2. Develop and implement capacity and awareness-raising activities on efficient water use, including low carbon emission practices.
3. Implementing capacity development activities specifically designed for women's empowerment in the context of the Future of Women in Iraq project in fields normally dominated by male representation.
4. Develop policies to be put into practice with the support of the private sector, such as providing incentives and subsidies to farmers who organize their water supply for agricultural purposes.

23. Despite these constraints, there are some positive aspects which can be capitalized upon to achieve the intended project goals. This includes a vibrant private sector in the food production system, a very strong commitment to policy reform in both countries, and the fact that water availability has improved significantly over recent years (and there is rapid increase of input suppliers who offer a wide range of climate-smart technologies). In addition, there are increasing efforts by governments to improve irrigation infrastructure and promote more efficient use of water in the agriculture sector as the largest user of water and assisting farmers in developing their capacity for water management. However, these efforts have been limited by support from the government in terms of protecting the sector from expropriations. About renewable energy, the project will focus on increasing the share of electricity generated from renewable sources to help ease the pressure on the country’s hydropower-dependent electricity plans. The GGI is also supporting the development and implementation of a national grid expansion plan (NREP) for the country (GGIN 2016). In one where a resident agricultural sector can also play its due part by its potential for employment generation, the project will support the private sector in developing and implementing climate-smart technologies. The project will also focus on adaptation or farmer concerns and are not equipped to deal with the growing demands of water, energy or climate risks. Therefore, it is important that governments and other stakeholders take a more proactive role in supporting farmers through management frameworks to support this country. However, more needs to be done in this regard and local communities and national authorities need to be involved in developing these frameworks. The project will also focus on the private sector as an engine of innovation and taking some bold measures that can help transition both the water and energy sectors and use of water and energy.

24. The project’s theory of change is premised on the assumption that “If farmers are made more aware of climate risks and have access to appropriate technologies, they will be better informed and empowered to play a more active role, capacity of water management institutions is strengthened through improved governance and institutional arrangements, and private sector actors are encouraged to adopt a gender-sensitive approach in the assessment of vulnerability and plan for men and women keeping in mind this issue. This will lead to increased water availability for women and men farmers; increased adoption of practices and technologies that improve productivity and incomes and reduce their costs.

25. The project’s theory of change is premised on the experience that there is a growing private sector that can be the engine of growth in the dissemination and supply of adaptive production techniques, efficient irrigation systems, and other climate-smart technologies. In addition, the private sector has been increasingly involved in scaling up these technologies have done so because of the growth of a vibrant and competitive market for agricultural inputs and services. The project will support the private sector to adopt a gender-sensitive approach in the assessment of vulnerability and plan for men and women keeping in mind this issue. This will lead to increased water availability for women and men farmers; increased adoption of practices and technologies that improve productivity and incomes and reduce their costs.

The TOC of the project recognizes that climate change affects men and women differently, and that women are more vulnerable than men in terms of access to resources and services. 27. The project’s theory of change is premised on the experience that there is a growing private sector that can be the engine of growth in the dissemination and supply of adaptive production techniques, efficient irrigation systems, and other climate-smart technologies. In addition, the private sector has been increasingly involved in scaling up these technologies have done so because of the growth of a vibrant and competitive market for agricultural inputs and services. The project will support the private sector to adopt a gender-sensitive approach in the assessment of vulnerability and plan for men and women keeping in mind this issue. This will lead to increased water availability for women and men farmers; increased adoption of practices and technologies that improve productivity and incomes and reduce their costs.

# Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

The objective of the project is to enhance the climate resilience of rural households through the introduction of climate-smart agriculture practices and technologies that will increase water availability, water efficiency, and secure agricultural yields at the farm level. The project is designed to increase water productivity per unit of production by improving irrigation infrastructure and increasing access to water for irrigation. It aims to promote a sustainable shift in the agriculture and energy sectors through investments at the farm and policy levels.

## Component 1: Strengthening resilience against climate-induced water scarcity

### Sub-Component 1.1: Investments in irrigation canals upgrading

Outcome 1: Increased water availability for women and men farmers

This component is designed to address water scarcity and variability due to climate change. The project evaluated deviation (Annex 16A–Figure 30) in the period between 1985 and 2020, with +5.09 mm/day; and +4.82 mm/day, respectively. Projections foresee a decrease of water availability by -7.0% for the period 2016-2040 (Annex 16A–Figure 30) and an increase in evaporation by +1.43 mm/sec; +0.29mm/sec; and +1.78 mm/sec, respectively (Annex 16A–Figure 41). There were several options for addressing the water scarcity problem, including shifting from open canals to closed systems, installing solar panels on canals that require less maintenance than traditional irrigation systems, and using drip irrigation systems. The project team evaluated these criteria outlined above and through discussions with key stakeholders on all the options. This option appeared to be the most suitable for the project area as it would provide a 100% increase in water availability (from 25.7 MCM per year to 51.7 MCM per year); reduced evaporation; no increased cost of operation and maintenance (0.75% compared to 2.94%) for lined canals, and would be more sustainable than other options.

No technologies or approaches were introduced that would lead to the extraction of more water from the ground or from canals. The project will not introduce any new irrigation systems but will upgrade existing ones. In case of investments in Solar energy resources, pumps and irrigation systems will be solely managed by the VUCA. The risk of over-extraction is therefore mitigated as it is already under control through the design of the project.

Sub-Component 1.2: Investments in water harvesting

## The primary goal of this component is to improve water conveyance efficiency and provide the country with the needed infrastructure for sustainable irrigation in the future.

The gravity fed canal system will show the same hydraulic principles of an open channel system but in a completely closed system, which will be more efficient than the existing open canals. This system will also reduce evaporation losses by up to 50% and carry larger flows; the material often used in Iraq (according to DAWR) is dacite iron. Installation is easy and safe for workers and farmers.

The gravity fed canal system will provide a more efficient irrigation delivery system that causes significant O&M work. The canal, Earthed or lined canals offer require significant O&M work to keep them functional and operational. This project will implement the necessary measures to minimize the potential for canal washouts which could leave users without irrigation water for a significant period while repairs are made.

Furthermore, shifting from open canals to piped ones will also increase the overall resilience of the infrastructure and it will be more efficient.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND VISION PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 18

## Implementation of this subcomponent will entail procuring a service provider and preparing engineering design documents for the irrigation system rehabilitation.

### Activities
- **Engineering design**
  - Prepare engineering designs, bills of quantities, cost estimates, and tender/procurement documents (consulting services). In close collaboration with all concerned stakeholders (i.e. government agencies, water users associations, etc.), prepare detailed engineering designs for the selected irrigation canals (works) to be rehabilitated/rehabilitated and constructed.
  - Prepare a tender document for the selection of a consulting firm (procured through an international bidding process) for design work that will establish elements necessary for the rehabilitation of the selected irrigation canals. The tender document should include a detailed schedule for the implementation of construction works and supplies, expected to last between 24-27 months with three lots (one for each canal). The lot sizes are as follows: Lot 1 - Canal 1; Lot 2 - Canals 2 & 3; Lot 3 - Canals 4 & 5. Work will be organized in such a way that the canals will be functional without long downtime.
- **Construction supervision**
  - Implement planned changes to selected irrigation canals (works). Repair, rehabilitate and construct the selected irrigation canals (works) as per the engineering designs prepared above. Interactive activities utilizing local labor inputs are encouraged.

### Description
Prepare engineering designs, bills of quantities, cost estimates and tender/procurement documents (consulting services). In close collaboration with all concerned stakeholders (i.e., government agencies, water users associations, etc.), prepare detailed engineering designs for the selected irrigation canals (works) to be rehabilitated/rehabilitated and constructed. Prepare a tender document for the selection of a consulting firm (procured through an international bidding process) for design work that will establish elements necessary for the rehabilitation of the selected irrigation canals. The tender document should include a detailed schedule for the implementation of construction works and supplies, expected to last between 24-27 months with three lots (one for each canal). The lot sizes are as follows: Lot 1 - Canal 1; Lot 2 - Canals 2 & 3; Lot 3 - Canals 4 & 5. Work will be organized in such a way that the canals will be functional without long downtime.

Based on experience, implementing partners will identify specific aspects related to refining water allocation policies for irrigation and drainage systems. The estimated cost of rehabilitation and construction works is USD 22.7 million (engineering design 6%, works 72%, construction supervision 8%, logistics 2%). The total number of beneficiaries is expected to be approximately 10,500 households. The project will include the installation of DDT-1 distributary canals and their watercourses (see Annex A Appendix 17). This preliminary design was used to establish an indicative budget for the rehabilitation of the pre-selected canals as shown in Table 7 above.

## 1.1.1 Operation and maintenance

Work on operation and maintenance will be managed throughout the whole process in the correct direction by the Government for the operation and management of the improved systems. After the installation, the responsibility for the operation and maintenance of the irrigation and drainage system will be transferred to the Water Users Association (WUA) or the Water Users Associations engaged by these WUAs. The responsibilities for operation and maintenance of the irrigation and drainage system will also be transferred to the Water Users Associations engaged by these WUAs.

## 1.1.1.3 Technical support and oversight

Sub-Component 1.2: Investments in Renewable Energy Systems

Output 1.2.1 Water canals covered with solar panels, providing 1,000 kW of renewable energy

40 Despite large investments in the energy sector, Iraq has not achieved full energy security (Annex 2, page 42)). Power cuts from 6 to 8 hours per day are common and electricity is often unreliable. This situation makes the country less reliable in rural areas). Solar energy potential is significant, especially in southern regions, but largely unexploited (Annex 2, page 13)). The project will support the installation of solar panels on water canals to provide a sustainable source of renewable energy for irrigation and drainage systems. This will increase energy security and therefore a more reliable distribution of water. Panels will be installed on top of recently lined water canals in rural agricultural areas while strengthening the Water-Energy-Food Nexus as a driver for sustainable rural development (Annex 2, page 13)). # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 28

## Activities

### Sub-Component 1.3: Investments in knowledge transfer, behavior change and training

#### Output 1.3.1 500 technical staff trained in design, installation and maintenance of irrigation, drainage and energy systems

**41. In Iran, there are no specialized vocational schools for irrigation and drainage, but there are departments of water resources technologies (irrigation and drainage techniques branch) at the level of technical institutes that provide a two-year curriculum after completion of secondary school education. The project will train 500 technical staff in design, installation and maintenance of water resources in colleges of engineering that train irrigation engineers, and the public sector has agricultural vocational schools where students are trained for three years to become agricultural technicians. The project will also train 100 teachers related to irrigation and drainage in the technical curricula of selected schools and develop the capacity of technical staff to teach these subjects at the level of secondary school education. The training will be organized through Training of Trainers activities, a module to be integrated in the curriculum of agricultural vocational schools and the technical institutes. This project highlights the importance of irrigation and drainage technologies for sustainable water use and energy applications. The Fund will cover all costs related to the organization of this process and the information disseminated through Training of the Trainers activities will be shared with other countries interested in similar training programs.**

**42. The capacity building of technical staff will be undertaken in three stages: an assessment to define the needs, followed by a training program that includes theoretical and practical sessions, and finally a follow-up phase aimed at ensuring the transfer and application of the knowledge acquired through this process. The training will provide practical knowledge related to design, installation and maintenance of irrigation and drainage technologies.**

**The project is also using participatory approaches across all its interventions that ensure sustainability through close collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The Fund will cover all costs related to the organization of these activities. 1995 a legal agreement will be sign between all the new WUA and the Directorate of Water Resources (DOWR) of the MWR as an operational unit for the design, installation and maintenance of hydraulic units in water fields. Therefore, after completion of the project, operation and maintenance of hydraulic units will become full responsibility of WUAs.**

The capacity building of technical staff will be undertaken in three stages: an assessment to define the needs, followed by a training program that includes theoretical and practical sessions, and finally a follow-up phase aimed at ensuring the transfer and application of the knowledge acquired through this process. The training will provide practical knowledge related to design, installation and maintenance of irrigation and drainage technologies.**

**The project is also using participatory approaches across all its interventions that ensure sustainability through close collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The Fund will cover all costs related to the organization of these activities. 1995 a legal agreement will be sign between all the new WUA and the Directorate of Water Resources (DOWR) of the MWR as an operational unit for the design, installation and maintenance of hydraulic units in water fields. Therefore, after completion of the project, operation and maintenance of hydraulic units will become full responsibility of WUAs.**

### Sub-Component 1.3: Investments in knowledge transfer, behavior change and training

#### Output 1.3.1 500 technical staff trained in design, installation and maintenance of irrigation, drainage and energy systems

**41. In Iran, there are no specialized vocational schools for irrigation and drainage, but there are departments of water resources technologies (irrigation and drainage techniques branch) at the level of technical institutes that provide a two-year curriculum after completion of secondary school education. The project will train 500 technical staff in design, installation and maintenance of water resources in colleges of engineering that train irrigation engineers, and the public sector has agricultural vocational schools where students are trained for three years to become agricultural technicians. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## 1.3.1 Capacity development of technical staff
Capacity building will be approached in three stages: holistic assessment to define the needs and design and implement a multi-year extensive capacity development program targeting the identified gaps and weaknesses, followed by training workshops and on-the-job training during this process. The training workshops are intended to enhance local capacity on how to operate and maintain the irrigation systems.

## 1.3.2 Update vocational schools and technical institutes' curricula
The project will also include updating the curriculum of agricultural vocational schools and the technical institutes informing about the advantages and possibilities for water savings technologies including solar energy, drip irrigation, etc., as well as the advantages and possibilities for water savings technologies including solar energy.

## Output 1.3.2.5 WUAs supported in developing and adopting more efficient and climate sensitive water distribution plans
The project will build on the work achieved with JICA assistance to develop the sustainable irrigation water management model in WUA model sites. The project will adopt and promote an approach that integrates social and technical aspects in modernizing the irrigation systems management in conjunction with Components 2 and 3. This presents a paradigm shift as it would involve not only the introduction of new technologies (e.g., solar-powered pumps, drip irrigation) but also the establishment of technical (hydraulic) units for the self-governing WUAs to establish and/or strengthen. Technical design would therefore require more than just the selection of appropriate technology; it will need to be accompanied by a package of institutional reforms reinforcing WUA members' technical and managerial capacities.

The potential for inclusion of women in these forums will be low, as they are mainly involved with household chores (See Annex E). In this regard, the project will build on the experience of JICA in the sector to adopt gender-sensitive approaches. The project will also ensure that women's rights and interests are protected by ensuring that women are part of a long-lasting plan of the State to modernize irrigation management.

The project will support WUAs with training on water distribution planning, including technical expertise. Specifically, a Technical Assistance (TA) package will be deployed in the targeted governorates related to:

- Water rights and duties
- Management of the Water Users Association, including management of Board meetings, General Assembly meetings, and the annual budget
- Infrastructure rehabilitation and transferment of any agreements
- Water distribution planning and water distribution systems management
- Water fee recovery and other sources of funding for WUAs
- Financial management
- WUA membership criteria (criteria of women based on usufruct); and
- Filing of documents

Based on this first step, the TA will also work at national level supporting the preparation of an appropriate legal framework for irrigation management by the WUAs within the framework of the existing Water Code. The TA mission will also include:

- Organize and conduct training in good-practice technical and irrigation scheduling with targets and outreach measures to ensure that all WUAs are covered
- Define the level of support needed at each WUA to insure implementation and respect of the scheme management code
- Assess and define the support, with block grants to each WUA, improvements of their facilities (buildings, equipment, etc.)

## 1.3.2 Reinvigorate WUA members' technical and managerial capabilities

The project will support the development of a training program that includes practical/technical and irrigation scheduling with targets and outreach measures to ensure participation in the training. The training will be conducted by extension staff and will cover: (i) water distribution plans based on crops requirements and climate change projections; (ii) crop rotation, intercropping, and agroforestry practices; (iii) soil conservation techniques and irrigation practices; and (iv) managing, operating, and maintaining irrigation infrastructure.

## Output 2.1.2. Enhanced capacity of 10,000 farmers in Climate Resilient Agriculture

58 It is expected that 10,000 farmers (3'000 women) will be trained through Farmers Field Schools (FFS). Implemented practices and technologies will allow better weather, crop and irrigation water use, understanding of soil characteristics, plant stress management, and the ability to adapt to climate change. The FFS will be implemented in a phased manner from start-up of the project and Output 2.1.2 is expected to showcase in this regard the practicality of investments in climate resilient agriculture. The FFS will also provide an opportunity for farmers to learn about the potential benefits of climate-smart agricultural practices, including mitigation benefits deriving from more productivity-oriented practices. Table 11 below reports how FFS will support farmers in the project area with respect to the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices (see Annexes 20 and 23). The FFS will be through the enhanced capacity of the technical extension staff who will continue to add top topics and security through the year. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 24

## Topic # Climate Change issues addressed

### Adaptation
- Farmers will be able to cope with the increased evaporation caused by increasing temperatures and reduced rainfall through improved irrigation methods, monitoring of soil water content, and better crop management.
- Improved irrigation methods will increase productivity per unit of water used while more stable yields can be achieved in dry years.

### Mitigation
- Shift to high carbon input and conservation agriculture improves the capacity of soils to act as a sink for atmospheric CO2. This is achieved by reducing soil tillage, increasing organic matter content through crop residue incorporation, and reducing the use of chemical fertilizers.
- Improved irrigation management will reduce water consumption while increasing yields.

### Training
- Training in improved irrigation methods with single-drip irrigation systems.
- Training on climate change adaptation techniques for farmers to cope with increased evaporation caused by increasing temperatures and reduced rainfall.

Sub - Component 2.2 Enhancing Awareness about Renewable Energy Applications for agriculture

Output 2.2.1 Technical capacities of 90 stakeholders and knowledge of at least 12,000 citizens on solar energy increased

53. Agriculture is a highly energy dependent sector, and some irrigation systems can only work with consistent and reliable energy supply. In areas where constant electricity is lacking or diesel fuel difficult to purchase or expensive, Solar Powered Irrigation Systems (SPIS) are an alternative solution. SPISs have been used in large-scale irrigation systems as well as for decentralized, small-scale irrigation and recognized as innovative mitigation technology. The project will provide training on the understanding of solar energy technologies, the design, installation, operation and maintenance of SPISs, the understanding and configuration of water demand (water requirements and enginale capacity) and supply side (the availability of solar radiation). The project will also disseminate information to farmers about the benefits of using renewable energy sources such as solar power for irrigation. The scope of the activities will include dissemination of climate sensitive technical innovations for SPIS based on the needs of the farmers in the selected governorates. The project will provide training and capacity building workshops to outsource the development of specialized trainings for extensionists and other stakeholders and awareness raising events to disseminate information about the benefits of using renewable energy sources such as solar power for irrigation. Investment opportunities: To increase awareness of the public on the technology, each year (from 12/13) one public event will be organized in collaboration with the local government authorities and the municipality of the selected governorates. For this purpose, a mobile training laboratory will be established and equipped with

### Sub-component 2.3: Enhancing Climate Resilience for Women
#### Output 2.3.1 A cadre of 150 Climate Wise Women (CWW) trained as change agents for climate adaptation

- Women in rural areas are disproportionately affected by climate change with unequal access to resources and assets.
- The CWW will be trained, mentored, and supported through a customized sixteen-week training delivered over the course of a year in 16 villages across Nepal. The training will be conducted by experienced trainers, practitioners, educators and influencers in climate adaptation and mitigation.
- Empowering rural women as agents of change is critical to building resilience at household level. Women have been trained on climate change adaptation and mitigation through various initiatives such as the CCAFS project implemented in 10 countries including Papua New Guinea, Uganda, Fiji, Nigeria, Maldives, Pakistan, Nepal, Indonesia, etc.
- The CCAP project will train women from three target governorates of Muthana, Jajarkot and Kaski. These young women will be advocates and repositories of knowledge on climate change adaptation.

- The women will be trained and certified through a customized sixteen-week training delivered over the course of a year in 16 villages across Nepal.
- The CWW will be developed further through mentorship, capacity building, and networking opportunities with other organizations. The CWW will also be supported by the AED, the Agricultural Extension Officers and strengthen the linkage between farmers and the AEDs. At the national level, it will highlight the role of women in climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- The CWW will be trained on various topics including: (a) Climate change adaptation and mitigation; (b) Supporting the behaviour change objectives project interventions such as (i) advocates and resource persons of climate adaptive agriculture for women and men farmers in their communities, (ii) training and mentoring of women and men farmers to develop and implement climate adaptive techniques on their own farm(s); facilitators of understanding and dialogue on climate change and modern climate technologies; (iii) linking the needs of smallholder farmers with government policies and plans through advocacy and lobbying.
- The CWW will be trained in the following areas: International and National Climate Adaptation and Gender & Social Inclusion Specialists. The project and gather data on the roles and responsibilities, their specific challenges and opportunities related to climate change and the lessons learnt from the CWW intervention.

## Activities

### Outcome 1: Technical Assistance for Climate Wise Women
- **Description:** Technical assistance will be provided to develop a short-course for training climate wise women change agents.

### Outcome 2: Develop Social and Behaviour Change Communication Strategy for Climate Wise Women
- **Description:** A Social and Behaviour Change Communication Strategy will be developed for branding, positioning and specifying lifestyle and behaviour change products for CWWW.

### Outcome 3: Designing Training Modules for Climate Wise Women
- **Description:** A service provider will be hired to conduct the training needs assessment and based on it a training module will be designed. The training modules will be piloted in the first quarter of Year 1.

### Outcome 4: Provide Training to Master CWWWs
- **Description:** Master CWWWs will be selected from post nominated by Directorate of Agriculture and Centres of Training and Extension and trained on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

### Outcome 5: Select and train CWWWs
- **Description:** A total of 100 women will be selected for training in a 12-week period in climate adaptation practices over the course of 18 months.

#### Output 2.3.4 40,000 women adapt to climate change
- **Description:** The project will make arrangements to support and supervise the beneficiary women through the MoU extension staff and other relevant stakeholders. The CWWWs will be trained in climate adaptation practices and will be supported by women farmers in their own villages. In addition, the CWWW will conduct community dialogues to understand the impact of climate change on households and its impacts on women. The CWWW will also work with the project team to identify and demonstrate gender sensitive climate resilience measures. It is expected that each CWWW will cover around four to six villages and will be supported by a village facilitator. The CWWWs will be trained in climate adaptation practices over the course of 18 months.

#### Activities
- **Outcome 2.3.1: Dissemination of climate resilient practices to CWWWs**
  - **Description:** The Climate Wise Women CWWW will hold dialogues with groups of women in the communities to enhance their awareness about climate change and how to cope with the risks associated with it.

- **Outcome 2.3.2: Organising multi-stakeholder events for CWWWs**
  - **Description:** The Climate Wise Women Forum will be organized in Year 1, 4 and 5 of the project. These events will serve to highlight the role of women as change agents, identify achievements and challenges faced by women in climate adaptation and mitigation activities and provide feedback to the Government of Iraq on actions required at multiple levels to address climate change.

### Component 3: Scaling-up climate adaptation through policy formulation and planning
- **Outcome 3.1: Policy environment for efficient water and energy management is enabled**
  - **Description:** In the context of policy formulation for the water and energy sectors, two main options were considered: the scenario under which the Government of Iraq (GoI) will continue to lead the GoI’s role in climate change adaptation and energy sector policies. These two options are outlined in Table A32 in Annex 2 of the Options Analysis section.

This component will also assist in ensuring alignment with the national adaptation plan and the national climate change action plan developed.

addressing the implementation of the Integrated National Energy Strategy that assigns a key role for providing energy security in rural areas to solar energy, and focuses, with the involvement and consultation of private sector actors, on the elaboration of a road map for investments in solar systems over water canals in the next decade. Activities


1.2.1 Develop a road map for solar systems over water canal identification
Description: This is an elaboration of the national energy plans, in line with the Integrated National Energy Strategy, that assigns a solar energy a key role for providing energy security in rural areas. The plan serves to also mobilize/activate parts of the hydroelectric potential and provide a road map for investments in the sector over the next decade. The plan contains clearly defined strategies and targets to be reached within the lifetime of the project and beyond. This will include an identification of the water canals that are suitable for solar systems, together with an assessment of the investment needs for rural electrification. Investments for the agricultural regions will be developed, together with a mapping and analysis of the potential for investments in off-grid and hybrid solutions. The plan will provide information to support informed and sound decision making for public and private investors.


B.A Implementation arrangements (1000 words approximately 3 pages of diagrams)


Accredited Entity (AE): FAO
65. FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project. FAO will be responsible for overall oversight of the Project, including but not limited to: a) Overall coordination and administrative financial and technical support throughout implementation of the Project; b) Supervision of effective management of funds to achieve the results and objectives; c) Quality control of Project outputs and outcomes; d) Ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with FAO’s rules, regulations, policies and procedures, including relevant requirements on fiduciary matters. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that the Organization is fully compliant with GCF and FAO rules and regulations, policies and procedures, including relevant requirements on fiduciary matters. The AE will assume these responsibilities in line with the detailed provisions listed in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF. The AMA will be signed by the Director General of FAO and the President of GCF. The AMA will be signed at the headquarters of FAO in Rome, Italy, as soon as possible after the signing of this Project Document.


66. To perform the AE functions, FAO will set up a dedicated TACO Group Task Force Project (TTF) (in line with FAO project management procedures). The TTF will be led by a Technical Advisor (TA) and will consist of a Chief Financial Officer (CFO), a Lead Technical Officer (LTO) and a Funding Liaison Officer (FLO). The TTF will remain independent from the Executive Board of FAO. The TTF will report to the Director General of FAO on all matters related to the Project. In accordance with GCF Board Decision 1985, the above-mentioned segregation of responsibilities within FAO will ensure that the Organization can independently and effectively perform the AE functions.


67. The TTF will be responsible for ensuring that the Project is carried out in accordance with the terms set forth in this Project Document, including the GCF General principles and indicative list of eligible costs covered under GCF fees and project management costs.


65. The Government of Iraq, through the Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR) and Ministry of Environment (MoE) will provide co-financing in the form of a in-kind contribution in the value of USD 23.3 million, and in form of in-kind contribution in the value of USD 10.5 million. The MoWR will be responsible for executing and managing their co-financing funds under the coordination of the Ministry of Environment. The MoE will be responsible for executing and managing their co-financing funds under the coordination of the Ministry of Water Resources. The MoWR and MoE will provide the in-kind contribution as outlined in the GCF policies as well as AMAs, Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between FAO and GCF and the co-financing agreement with the Government of Iraq. The MoWR and MoE will be responsible for executing and managing their co-financing amount. This co-financing agreement with the Government of Iraq (covering the co-financing from MoWR and MoE) will be signed by the Director General of FAO and the President of GCF. The MoWR and MoE will be responsible for executing and managing their co-financing funds under the coordination of the Ministry of Environment will be responsible for executing and managing their co-financing funds under the coordination of the Project

Management Unit (PMU) and through the Project Steering Committee. FAO will provide co-financing in the form of a grant contribution in the value of USD 8.6 million and in form of in-kind at the value of USD 0.01million. Details are provided in Annexure 4.

Other Government Agencies Involvement

In addition to the cofinancing provided by MoHRR and MoA, also work in very close collaboration with other Government of Iraq entities such as the Ministry of Water, agriculture extension services and solar irrigation systems. The Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) and its affiliated Directorates in the field will assist with the facilitation of activities in the project Governments of Najaf, Karbala and Muthanna will provide support to the implementation of the project. The Iraqi Environment Ministry (IEM) will be responsible for ensuring compliance with the regulatory framework for RES. The IEM will be expected to track and study the lessons generated by the project for wider application in Iraq.

Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established for the overall strategic guidance of the project and housed at the Ministry of Environment (MoE). The PSC will be co-chaired by the FAO Representative to Iraq and the MoE. The MoE, as the lead agency, will provide secretariat support to the PSC. The PSC will meet on a regular basis to review progress against project milestones, assess performance and issue appropriate guidance. The PSC will be composed of primary stakeholders such as the Ministry of Water (MoW), the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), the Iraqi Environment Ministry (IEM) and the MoE. The PSC will also include representatives from the Iraq Ministry of Electricity (IME), and the Governors of Najaf, Karbala and Muthanna. The Steering Committee will meet on a regular basis to review progress against project milestones, assess performance and issue appropriate guidance.

## Implementation Arrangement and Reporting

### Executing Entities

68. The MoU in its capacity as the NDA and as the lead agency responsible for environmental uses will act as the executing entity of the GCF funds and its co-financing.

The Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR) and Ministry of Environment (MoE) will be the executing entities of their own component, which will be executed through their respective departments. The MoWR will work with 4 Ministries (Water Resources, Agriculture and Electricity). None of the involved ministries have any other GCF projects in their pipeline or are co-financing any other GCF projects.

As such, FAO will sign with the GoI a sub-award agreement which will detail the roles and responsibilities of each party. The MoWR and MoE will be responsible for the execution of the project components under their respective portfolios. The MoWR will also be responsible for executing the selected activities under various components through technical experts in the PMU or other implementing partners on a full-time or part-time basis.

71. To perform its Executing Entity functions, FAO will set up a Project Management Unit (PMU), in Nepal which is one of the key project components. The PMU will be established and managed under the overall supervision of the NDA and will be led by a Project Coordinator/Implementation Specialist. The PMU will be supported by technical experts assigned to each technical component, as required.

The PMU will be responsible for the day-to-day management of the project, including planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, reporting, and financial management on a full-time or part-time basis.

### Figure 7 Flow of Funds

The Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR) has been closely involved in the design of the project and will support in coordinating activities associated with components 1 and 3, which includes the conversion of open canals to closed canals, the rehabilitation of irrigation systems, and the installation of solar water pumps. The MoWR will also be responsible for staff and policy review. The PMU in association with the MoWR will coordinate activities with respect to the investments in the irrigation systems and the solar water pumps. The selection of the contracting companies will be undertaken through a competitive process in which both the public and private companies will be invited to bid. The same procedure will also be applied for the commissioning of the rehabilitated systems and of the solar installations. The PMU will hire technical specialists to strengthen the capacity of the MoWR staff on water management, irrigation, and solar energy technologies. The MoWR will also be involved in the policy for improving the system of water allocation in discussions with key stakeholders together with the FAO. The MoWR will also be responsible for the selection of the contracting companies for the commissioning of the rehabilitated systems and of the solar installations. The PMU will hire technical specialists to strengthen the capacity of the MoWR staff on water management, irrigation, and solar energy technologies.

The Ministry of Agriculture will identify and train its own staff in extension service delivery and training by the Master Trainers. The FAO will provide support for organizing field visits and workshops for farmers trained by the Master Trainers. The PMU will be responsible for organizing the Field Visit Days (FVDs) for farmers, for which FAO has provided the necessary technical materials and supplies. FAO and MoA work closely together to ensure that the FVDs are organized in a way that allows farmers to learn about the most appropriate technologies for their area.

The Department of Agriculture at Government level will assist in organizing FGD field days. This includes providing information on the latest research findings, agricultural research stations and technical experts to demonstrate crop management practices and techniques to a wider audience.

The National Gender Office (NGO) with qualified technical specialists for designing and delivering the training of Master Trainers and Climate Wise Women. The NGO will also provide support in organizing field visits and workshops for farmers trained by the Master Trainers; (i) identify a team of potential Climate Wise Women from the selected Governmentates, (ii) organize training of the selected women on climate change adaptation techniques, (iii) monitor their progress during the implementation period, and (iv) identify innovative opportunities for their continued deployment at the end of the project and (v) organize CWFs workshops to disseminate lessons learned from the experience. Activities, list of beneficiaries and training curricula will be reviewed and validated by FAO;traing materials will be provided by FAO.

The funds from GCF will be transferred only to FAO for the purpose of flow directly to the EEs. Each EE is responsible for the activities under its responsibility with its own funding (co-financing). There will be no transfer of funds from the GCF or FAO to the EEs.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Iraq economy has faced significant challenges in recent years due to high inflation rates of 76% and 43% of its gross domestic product (GDP) in 2019. The Iraqi economy has been confronted with a persistent decline in oil prices, which have fallen from $85 per barrel in 2014 to $35 per barrel as of March 2022. The conflict in Syria, the needs of internally displaced persons and the negative impact of the COVID-19 pandemic has led to further pressure on the economy.

The Iraq economy is heavily dependent on oil production, which accounts for approximately 80% of government revenue and 43% of its gross domestic product (GDP) in 2019. **B**

### Identified private sector engagement as key to delivering on its climate future.

This is explicitly expressed in Iraq's Private Sector Development Strategy (2013-2030). The document provides priorities across the high potential sectors: namely agriculture, energy, water and industry. Iraq needs urgent investment in the agricultural sector due to its contribution to GDP and employment which are both heavily dependent on agriculture. Iraq is also highly vulnerable to climate change impacts as it relies heavily on surface water resources (Tigris and Euphrates). Climate change impacts the water sector, in turn impacting the agricultural sector. The country has experienced a significant increase in temperature over recent years with increased precipitation patterns together with increasing evaporation are causing water stress in Iraq both in rained and irrigated areas.

The rural population already suffering from social unrest by focusing on adaptation technologies within agriculture and water sectors. This will also contribute to the diversification of income sources, improving resilience and reducing poverty levels. The project will focus on identifying and implementing climate resilient agricultural practices and restoring agricultural and water systems that support smallholder farming families to diversify incomes, increase resilience, and reduce poverty.

79. Based on a study by the MoA, Iraq may need a direct significant higher percentage of its budget to water resources development in order to adapt to climate change impacts. The cost of implementing new irrigation technologies and promoting adaptive climate practices would require more than $12 billion from now until 2035. This is in addition to the costs associated with maintaining existing infrastructure. In light of the poor state of many of existing infrastructures that have been severely damaged during the past years of conflict, GCF assistance will enable the country to address these issues and ensure that Iraq can continue to provide food security for its population.

The project will focus on identifying and implementing climate resilient agricultural practices and restoring agricultural and water systems that support smallholder farming families to diversify incomes, increase resilience, and reduce poverty levels. The project will also contribute to the diversification of income sources, improving resilience and reducing poverty levels by focusing on adaptation technologies within agriculture and water sectors.

80. The grant from GCF will be used to make investments in public irrigation infrastructure as well as to enhance the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers through the provision of climate resilient agricultural practices. This is because financial sector credit is reluctant to lend to the agriculture sector because of the risks associated with the sector which are growing due to climate change impacts and extreme weather events. The project will focus on identifying and implementing climate resilient agricultural practices and restoring agricultural and water systems that support smallholder farming families to diversify incomes, increase resilience, and reduce poverty levels.

The grant from GCF will be used to make investments in public irrigation infrastructure as well as to enhance the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers through the provision of climate resilient agricultural practices. This is because financial sector credit is reluctant to lend to the agriculture sector because of the risks associated with the sector which are growing due to climate change impacts and extreme weather events.

81. Most of the targeted households in rural areas are female-headed households. Small-scale farming households changing to more efficient water management practices and resilient agricultural practices will be able to increase their income levels, improve food security for themselves and their families, and that declines in land and biomass productivity affects women more than men, especially in rural areas. SRAVII has been designed with this fact, and thus mainstreams gender perspectives and mitigates gender resource scarcity as it is one of its components.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section assesses the performance of the green climate fund against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's initial Investment Framework.

## D.1. IMPACT POTENTIAL (MAX. 500 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

The project is expected to contribute to a number of GCF targets identified in the strategic program 2024-2027 as indicated in Table 19.

| USTP target | Main contribution |
| --- | --- |

| 3. Ecosystems | Improved water availability and use of 870 ha IRR (as expected mitigation co-benefit) |
| 4. Infrastructure | Water supply will be brought under sustainable management practices |
| 7. Clean energy | All 60 km irrigation canals will be rehabilitated in a climate resilient manner |

### Project Design

The project is designed to enhance climate resilience and sustainable agriculture households in Iraq's rural communities in Karbala, Najaf and Muthanna with a strong focus gender. It is expected that the project will contribute for the following GCF targets: 1.2 million hectares of land under improved irrigation systems; rehabilitation of water distribution systems; stabilizing and increasing water availability at the farm level; increasing crop water productivity by 30%; improving access to climate information services; strengthening local water management institutions and the water distribution regime; reducing the adaptation deficit of farming communities via the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices; and enhancing the capacity of farmers' associations. The project will address water needs and other barriers at farm level, combining investments in improved irrigation systems and technologies with a focus on gender equality and social inclusion.

The project is expected to directly benefit 105,900 households (32% of all farming households) and indirectly benefit an additional 448,600 households through direct and indirect employment. The project will benefit 711,900 women in the project area (WPA). It is expected that between 11% and 12% of the households in the project area (WPA) Tables 1, 2 and 3 in Annex B provide details on the number of households by gender. The project will also benefit 750 farmers' associations through technology to disseminate information on weather, climate related hazards and dissemination of practices and technologies for adaptation and resilience building. In addition, 700,000 people from farming households are expected to benefit indirectly because of the national extension service provided by the Ministry of Agriculture. In addition, around 700,000 people from farming households are expected to benefit indirectly because of the national extension service provided by the Ministry of Agriculture.

It is important to highlight that the activities included in component 3 working with government institutions for planning and implementation of climate change adaptation and resilience building at the local level. In addition, policies are adopted and implemented which could be beyond the scope and implementation timeline of the project. The project will also contribute towards the achievement of the GCF targets by providing immediate support in terms of promoting climate change adaptation and resilience building via scaling and replicating the practices and technologies developed under the project. This can be achieved through replication of best practices, doing so and by constantly monitoring the execution of FFS and ETACC activities; the project will provide immediate support to 10,000 farmers while enabling the state via its extension service officer to replicate and

## Scaling up climate solutions in both project areas and across communities

This approach will allow to expand and replicate project impacts beyond project areas and across communities.

### Project Impacts on Indigenous Peoples

It is expected that the project will benefit indigenous groups and peoples that will be impacted by project activities. It is considered unlikely that the target governments host indigenous Peoples; however, before implementing project activities in the three target Governments, the project will ensure that the rights of the affected indigenous peoples are respected.

### Gender Action Plan

The project aims to empower rural women in the three target Governments through giving them visibility as farmers and supporting their participation in decision-making. The project will also support women’s access to land and credit for agricultural practices, providing opportunities for decision-making and leadership. The Gender Action Plan of the project ensures that women are included in all aspects of the project.

### Renewable Energy

The project will have an impact in terms of reducing GHG emissions (Core Indicator 1). Solar-powered irrigation systems will be installed to reduce water consumption and increase efficiency, thereby reducing GHG emissions from irrigation. The project will transfer the positive experience of several countries using solar panels which are installed on irrigation canals. Given the high potential for renewable energy generation in the three target Governments, this component will serve the dual objective of providing reliable energy to farmers and avoiding CO2 emissions. The renewable energy generated by these systems will be used to power irrigation pumps or other agricultural equipment.

### Water Management

The project will have an impact on reducing GHG emissions (Core Indicator 1). Solar-powered irrigation systems will be installed in remote areas, where supply of electricity from the grid is not guaranteed and diesel is often being used for pumping water. The project will transfer the positive experience of several countries using solar technologies that will increase productivity without increasing water consumption to provide a more efficient and sustainable use of water resources. This component will have an impact on reducing GHG emissions in two ways: (a) reduced water pumping needs; (b) installation and use of solar panels on irrigation canals (c) introduction of climate-resilient management practices.

### Carbon Sequestration

The project will have an impact on increasing carbon sequestration (Core Indicator 2). The project will implement a number of activities in the rice sector that are expected to increase soil organic matter and improve good agricultural practices. It is estimated that at least 1,344,154 tonnes of CO2eq will be avoided over 30 years through these activities.

### Cost-Benefit Analysis

The cost per tonne of avoided CO2eq in the project is about USD 28 (Annex 2). The cost to well below that of other crosscutting and integrated climate change mitigation measures. The project will also implement a number of direct mitigation measures for the implementation of the mitigation of GHG emissions in the rice sector that foresee an improved irrigation of the crop, which is expected to reduce water consumption by up to 30% (Annex 2). This would result in a 15-year reduction. The lessons learned of these activities are especially important to scale up mitigation action in other countries and regions where similar conditions prevail.

### Project Costs

The total cost of the project and direct mitigation cost and mitigation co-benefits, the cost per tonne of avoided CO2eq is about USD 28 (Annex 2). The cost to well below that of other crosscutting and integrated climate change mitigation measures. The project will also implement a number of direct mitigation measures for the implementation of the mitigation of GHG emissions in the rice sector that foresee an improved irrigation of the crop, which is expected to reduce water consumption by up to 30% (Annex 2).
