## Concluding Remarks

Cautiously irrigated systems with PV panels would also save water due to the reduction of water evaporation. Evaporation rates from irrigation channels may vary and can be as high as 80% in the case of open channels as 5–20 mm/day (Fulford, et al., 1984). The effect of various sharing methods on the evaporation rate was recently investigated by the CGIAR-CCAFS project. The results show that the reduction can be as high as 30% and as low as ranging from 40% to 80%. The exact amount of savings depends on several parameters such as how the modes are managed, the type of irrigation system used, and the crop being irrigated. It is estimated that a total of 121,965 hectares of farming area will be tracked by the DOWM at the Government level and reported during reporting periods.

It is estimated that 121,965 hectares of farming area will be brought under climate-resilient management practices in the three target Governments. The project will also support the improved irrigation systems with water meters, and including the land managed by the farmers attending the Farmer

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words). Approximately 1 page)

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

The primary goal of the rehabilitation of the canal infrastructure in component 1 is to provide a reliable water supply system to the country with the needed technical skills to replicate activities in other irrigated areas of Iraq. The acquired expertise will therefore be essential for the successful implementation of the project. The Government has already expressed its willingness to scale up and replicate the performance of the piped system will encourage the Government to make the investment despite its limited fiscal capacity. The Government is also considering the possibility of establishing a public-private partnership (PPP) to install solar photovoltaic systems (SPVs) on existing irrigation canals to implement technology that can be scaled up in other areas of Iraq. SPV installations on irrigation canals provide a cost-effective option which can then be scaled up with partnership with private sector investors. The project will also facilitate the installation of solar-powered irrigation pumps for SPV installations on irrigation canals and provide a cost-effective option which can then be scaled up with partnership with private sector investors. Utilizing water infrastructure for SPVS installations is expected to create significant savings in terms of energy costs, as well as reduce the need for additional land acquisition costs. The project will include the CWN more formally into the extension system of government to create networks of support. The private sector will be encouraged to invest in irrigation systems and provide technical assistance to farmers who have an incentive to increase its use of adaptive inputs. The project will actively facilitate the linkages and network between public and private sectors, as well as between different stakeholders. The knowledge acquired and applied within the project will be disseminated thanks to the TNCs and integrated in the national agricultural extension system. The project is expected to have a significant impact on women farmers who are powerful agents of sustainable development.

#### Knowledge sharing and learning

The project, in particular through its Component 1 and 2, will invest in training and capacity development activities for public stakeholders, farmers, extensionists and the population of the FVS. The CWN will be able to provide a platform for knowledge sharing and learning on best practices introduced by the project will be sustained over time at the national level. The SCICAD system aimed at disseminating information on climate change adaptation strategies and technologies is expected to be an important tool in this regard.

#### In addition, a crucial contribution to knowledge sharing is expected through activities involving Climate Wise Women Farmers (CWWF) who are powerful agents of sustainable development as well as trade unions and women's organizations. The project will also facilitate the establishment of networks between public and private sector extension specialists throughout the country.

addition, solar panels installed on top of canals can also reduce annual evaporation by an average of 39 to 12 liters/m² per year of canal (Mullins et al., 2021). The approach can be combined with inverted river regulation. In this case, the canal water level is raised and solar panels are installed on top of the canal. This has multiple benefits, due to the unobstructed flow of water in the canal. Case studies of over-canal solar photovoltaic arrays have already been conducted (e.g., in India). The results show that the shade provided by the solar panels can reduce evaporation and mitigate heat stress for livestock. In addition, the presence of pollutants on the natural ecosystems, and unsuitable use of synthetic chemical fertilizers and pesticides are also reduced.

The frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heat waves, and cold spells will increase. The growth cycles of winter crops (FAO-GESF, 2019) Desiccation and water scarcity due to erratic rainfall patterns and the increasing frequency of dry spells will reduce the productivity of rainfed agriculture. The frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heat waves, and cold spells will increase. The frequency of heat waves will increase, and heat stress is expected to be more frequent in the next five years (WMO, 2018). This will have a negative impact on livestock production.

The levels of water salinity and soil salinity, especially in the Euphrates River basin, are high and are expected to increase in the future. The increasing frequency of droughts and floods will lead to increased soil erosion and sedimentation increases, and efficient irrigation techniques are crucial to maintain productivity and food security (Sajjani et al., 2018). In addition, climate change is expected to reduce the quality of water resources by up to 30% in some areas. Another 25% has levels of salinity that have converted once productive lands into salt-affected wastelands. It is a matter of concern for the future sustainability of agriculture and food security.

The scenario indicates future threats on coastlines due to sea level rise. This is decreasing land productivity, diminishing water availability, and increasing soil erosion. The coastal areas are also vulnerable to flooding and storm surges that have an impact on agriculture for their livelihoods. This figure reaches around 40% in the southern governorates. Farmers of saline and alkaline soils face a number of challenges such as low yields, poor quality produce, and high production costs caused cropping systems to move away from high-value crops to lower-value crops. It is estimated that 53% of the population living on the coastlines are vulnerable to climate change impacts (UNDP, 2018). This will lead to migration to cities, accelerate urbanization and intensify pressure on already strained and degraded economic and social infrastructure.

In addition to changes in climate patterns, the critically important Euphrates and Tigris River basins are faced with increasing water scarcity. The basin is one of Iraq’s most productive agricultural regions, but it faces quality concerns and recent and ongoing conflicts which will deeply affect future water availability in the basin (GIZ, 2018). In addition to the existing challenges, climate change will exacerbate these problems by reducing the greater part of irrigated agriculture. This will lead to a decrease in the quality of water resources and continue to benefit upstream users to the detriment of Iraq. There are currently no transboundary riparian agreements in place for the Euphrates-Tigris river basins. The lack of such agreements has led to conflicts over water use which includes damage to water systems, irrigation facilities and other agricultural infrastructure, disruption of value chains and loss of livelihoods. In addition, climate change will have a significant impact on the availability of land for innovation and climate proofing especially in the agriculture sector, as a result of which many of the farming households have no access to land or are unable to use it effectively due to lack of irrigation infrastructure and other production technologies key. At all the same time the government needs to overcome the barrier of a lack of supportive policies and institutional frameworks that can help farmers adapt to climate change.

The transformation climate and environment related strategy to respond to the growing climate risks

There is an urgent need to invest in converting open irrigation systems to piped systems, building field channels to reduce runoff losses, and increasing water use efficiency. This will be achieved by promoting the usage of more efficient irrigation methods (e.g., drip irrigation, sprinkler irrigation), the promotion of crops such as wheat, barley, sorghum, and millet which are more drought-tolerant than other crops, and the introduction of low-cost or disease-resistant through the private sector tested crop species and varieties (especially for wheat resistant to stripe rust) and the development of new varieties. The government should also encourage the agricultural community at scale for adoption. Climate change trends will need to be monitored for ensuring awareness and access to information on climate change.

In rural areas, women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to resources such as land, water, and credit. Women in rural areas face greater food insecurity as well as more barriers to education, formal employment opportunities, and political participation (UNDP, 2018). Women are especially impacted by the effects of climate change; in a drought situation, women and girls bear the increased burden of fetching water and facilitating other basic household needs from longer distances as water resources dry up.

### D.5 Country ownership (max. 350 words, approximately 1 page)

Existing national climate strategy

The country has been taking strong ownership by trying to address the challenges it faces with respect to climate risks over the last decade despite the political instability and the turmoil in the country. Farmers in Iraq are suffering from a number of problems including water scarcity, soil salinity, desertification, droughts, floods, shortages of irrigation and lack of appropriate technologies. The agricultural sector is in highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to its dependence on rainfall for irrigation and the fact that it is located in the arid regions where increased vulnerability of rural communities. The predicted future climate conditions will significantly reduce water availability and increase evaporation rates, which will lead to a decrease in soil moisture content, reduced precipitation, increased evapotranspiration, and decreased soil moisture, and increased soil salinity. The country has been taking strong ownership by trying to address the challenges it faces with respect to climate risks over the last decade despite the political instability and the turmoil in the country.

The country ratified the UNFCCC in 2008. The MoE worked with UNDP, GIZ, and the Kyoto Protocol Secretariat (KPS) in the preparation of national communications for the first time under the UNFCCC. Iraq has signed the Paris Agreement on climate change in December 2016 but yet to ratify the agreement. The country is also a party to the Kyoto Protocol. The National Environment Strategy and Action Plan for Iraq (2013-2017) includes the provisions of the UNFCCC. The National Environmental Strategy and Action Plan for Iraq (2013-2017) includes the provisions of the UNFCCC. The Government of Iraq has been committed to the cause of climate change and has taken several actions in this regard.

The MoE has committed to the development of a national strategy for adapting impacts of climate change, noting that the country is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its dependence on rainfall for irrigation and the fact that it includes working with regional countries to adopt a regional programme to combat drought, dust storms and desertification. The MoE has also been taking several actions in this regard. In addition, the Ministry of Agriculture and other relevant ministries have also taken several actions to protect Iraq's environment, especially its water resources. These include: (i) developing a national strategy for adapting impacts of climate change; (ii) regarding the integrated framework for drought risk management; (iii) forming a committee with the assistance of UNDP to prepare a draft law on desertification and land degradation; (iv) establishing a system for sustainable development (SDS) and it is in cooperation with UNEP to train local cadres in desalination issues and SDS meet the requirements of the national strategy for preparation of strategies, programs, national legislations and projects to mitigate desertification.

**115.** There are three readiness projects which have been signed between Iraq and GCF between 2017 and 2018, and the total value of these projects is USD 9 million. These projects will be executed through the NDA. This will enable the Ministry of Environment as a national focal point for the GCF, and the UNDP to work in an integrated manner with the Iraqi government on the implementation of the project. The first is the Strategic Approval Process (SAP) for Iraq for building adaptation capacity of vulnerable households. The total value of projects under this SAP is USD 5 million. The second is the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Building Development to the Adaptation Fund for Building Resilience of the Agricultural Sector to Climate Change (BRAC). The details of the GCF projects are given in Table 21.

| Table 21 | List of GCF Projects in Iraq |
| --- | --- |
| Project Name | Type | Duration (in Type) | UNDP | Readiness |
| NDA Strengthening & Country Programming | UNDP | 14/09/2017 to 24 | Readiness | $68,356 |
| SAP for Iraq for Building adaptation capacity of vulnerable households in Southern Iraq | WFP | 14/09/2017 to 24 | SAP | $1.5 million |

**116.** NDA Strengthening & Country Programming-UNDP: The NDA Strengthening & Country Programming proposal was approved in November 2019 and is now being executed by UNDP. The project will support the Government of Iraq in strengthening the national capacities to effectively access and efficiently manage, climate change adaptation funds. The project will also provide technical assistance for the establishment of a National Designated Authority (NDA), strengthen stakeholders' engagement and effective participation in the GCF process, and develop a national strategy on climate change adaptation and mitigation priorities.

**117.** SAP for Iraq for Building Adaptation Capacity of Vulnerable Households-UNDP: The project will be executed by WFP in along with the Ministry of Environment. The Ministry of Agriculture is expected to support government efforts to provide technical assistance to vulnerable households, particularly women-headed households whose livelihoods are at risk from climate change. The project interventions will focus on three governorates (Dhi Qar, Wasit and Maysan) where the most vulnerable communities live. The project will aim to enhance agricultural productivity through the promotion of stress tolerant seed varieties; foster multi-level efforts for reducing post-harvest losses by providing storage facilities; and promote diversification through provision of climate-resilient economic assets. The project will capture knowledge on climate science, adaptation strategies and technologies that are relevant in Iraq.

**118.** Building Resilience of the Agricultural Sector to Climate Change (BRAC)-UNDP: This proposal has not yet been approved and no work has started on the ground. The project is being implemented through UNIDO. This proposal was approved in November 2019 and seeks to provide technical guidance and support for the development of a National Designated Authority (NDA) for Building Resilience of the Agricultural Sector to Climate Change (BRAC). The project will also develop an action plan aimed at creating a categorization and prioritization of mitigation and adaptation technologies that will be implemented in Iraq. The project will also aim to establish a Technical Committee (TC) for establishing a NDA, contribute directly to ongoing country programming process, establish a coordination mechanism between the GCF and other climate change funds, and develop a pipeline of projects to be submitted to the GCF. High level political support has been initiated within the policy decision making bodies in Iraq with the aim of ensuring that the project will have the necessary resources to implement key strategies. A key deliverable for the project will be the identification of a number of project ideas in the field.

**119.** Adaptation Platform-UNEP: The objective of the proposed project approved in December 2019 was to advance the implementation of Iraq’s National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and ensure that climate change adaptation mainstreaming will be maintained into national and local planning. This will ensure that NAP implementation is successful, and that there are no gaps between the NAP and other sectoral plans. The project will also involve stakeholders in ongoing adaptation actions. The project is also expected to provide coordination and synergy at the sub-national level for climate change adaptation efforts. The project will aim to achieve four key outputs: (i) National and sectoral systems and processes are strengthened; (ii) Climate change adaptation strategies, plans and projects are developed and the process assessed and bridged; (iii) National Adaptation Plans developed; and (iv) Funding strategy for the implementation of climate change adaptation investments in Iraq. Alignment with existing policies such as NDCs, NAMAs, and NAPs

130. In 2020, Iraq worked with UN Environment and other partners to present its Initial National Communication to the UNFCCC. This document outlines a series of national programmes, strategies and laws that Iraq has undertaken or is implementing in order to address climate change impacts and vulnerabilities, including adaptation measures and mitigation measures, calling for financial and technical support for implementation.

The Iraqi government committed itself to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 10% below the business-as-usual scenario by 2030. The project is in line with the new objectives that increase the conditional GHG emission reduction target to 10% compared to the baseline of 2015, and the unconditional target to 48%. Iraq has also committed itself to increasing its renewable energy capacity from 67 MW in 2019 to 3 GW by 2030. The majority of this should be achieved through the installation of 12 GW of renewable energy power. To increase the resilience and adaptability of the country, Iraq is implementing a series of adaptation measures for water infrastructure and prepare the water sector for future increasing demand and potential deficit by applying an integrated approach to water resources management.

In order to achieve these objectives, Iraq has developed a National Adaptation Programme (NAP) as part of this project. Iraq NDC aims to attract new investment opportunities and provide more engagement for private sector in energy projects. The NDC also includes the development of a climate change adaptation strategy that will update the outputs of I.2.1 with private sector investment as a contribution to the NDC. This topic will be discussed further during the workshop.

The Iraqi government has been working closely with UN Environment and other partners, including the World Bank, to encourage public and private investment in RES. Iraq experts and officials have discussed and agreed on an outline for the development of a climate change adaptation strategy that will update the outputs of I.2.1 with private sector investment as a contribution to the NDC.

process, the role of Government authorities should be strengthened so that medium- and long-term adaptation opportunities can be identified, implemented, and monitored at Government level. The Government of Iraq does not currently have a comprehensive system for monitoring and evaluation of climate change-related initiatives at national or state local levels. This is consequently limited knowledge regarding the strengths and weaknesses of these initiatives, as well as their impact on people’s livelihoods. Furthermore, many of these initiatives nor have they been incorporated into revisions of existing or new initiatives. Currently, only externally led project-based monitoring and evaluation systems are in place. The establishment of a national-level monitoring and evaluation system would provide a systematic approach to reviewing all climate change-related initiatives undertaken by the Government of Iraq. This would also help to ensure that the lessons learned from these initiatives can be used for future decision-making and advancing the NAP process in Iraq.

### 129.
A set of financial models were elaborated to assess the project's expected financial results in farms. Crops considered are wheat, barley, sorghum, maize, rice and cotton. The models used for the analysis are: (i) a crop model based on the average yield per hectare; (ii) a farm-level model based on the average yields per water productivity, following the current cropping pattern in Napul, Muthanna and Kirkuk. Models used the available data from 2013-2014 for Napul and 2015-2016 for Muthanna and Kirkuk.

### 130.
The project also demonstrated a positive Economic rate of return of 16.06% under the base case scenario (excluding environmental benefits). The economic profitability was estimated at 5% and the social profitability was estimated as 4% given the Central Bank of Iraq current policy interest rate Table 23 below). The economic profitability is based on the incremental profit per household, which is the difference between the incremental revenue and costs. Incremental benefits, with an adoption rate of 60%, were compared with total project costs (excluding investment costs and environmental schemes) estimated at USD 145 million. The social profitability was calculated using shadow prices for water (USD 2.5 per cubic meter) and land (USD 30 per hectare). Shadow prices are used to estimate the economic value of resources that are not directly traded in markets, such as water and land. Environmental schemes estimated at USD -144,154 equivalent CO2 avoided on the benefits lifecycle (with shadow price for water USD 2.5 low shadow price scenario). More references on parameters are presented in Annex 3 EFA Spreadsheet.

### Sensitivity Analysis was undertaken using different risk-occurrence scenarios. These included an increase in project costs (10% and 20%), a reduction in project benefits (10% and 20%), and combined scenarios (of both benefits and costs) of 50% every two years due to the occurrence of climate change shocks were considered.

The sensitivity test was also linked to the key risks and other key variables. Detailed assumptions and calculations are attached in Annexes 3 and 4. Table 24 below presents the main general results of the sensitivity test.

### Activities Description Sub-activities Deliverables

#### 1.1.1 Engineering design
Prepare engineering designs, bills of quantities, cost estimates and tender documents (consulting services). In close collaboration with all concerned stakeholders (government, farmers and water users), undertake feasibility studies and prepare a detailed project document for fast tracking of repair, rehabilitation and construction works.
- Draft ToRs for consulting firm to conduct the detailed technical study
- Conduct the bidding process to select the consulting firm to do the detailed technical study
- Prepare detailed design and bidding documents
- Approve the studies by the contracting authority

A descriptive report. This report must explain in detail what has been done, the data taken for the execution of the studies and the conclusions drawn from them.
A calculation note: this note should contain all the calculations made during the feasibility study.
A list for execution plans: The file must contain all plans, profiles and drawings which will be used as a reference to execute the topographic surveys carried out
A list with the description and location of the geotechnical studies as needed.

#### 1.1.2 Construction supervision
Provide services for control and supervision of construction works.
- Assist the contracting authority in conducting the process work
- Assisting the contracting authority for the control and supervision of construction works

Periodic reports (weekly, monthly, site visits) to be submitted by the consultant according to the progress of the work and as required.

#### 1.1.3 Construction works
Implement the planned changes to the selected irrigation infrastructure through design and construct selected irrigation infrastructure through phased implementation activities, utilizing local labor inputs as much as possible.
- Execute the construction contract(s) under the supervision and control of the consultant
- Prepare the manual of operation and maintenance of the constructed system
- Proceed by the reception of the construction works by the contracting authority

Construction works completed as per the specifications and drawings (including all kinds of drainage canals and their associated structures, closed pipeline system)

#### 1.1.4 Operation and maintenance
Water User Associations will be involved throughout the operation and management of the improved irrigation infrastructure. The WUA will manage the operation and maintenance of the improved irrigation infrastructure as per the manual prepared by these WUAs. The responsibilities for operation and maintenance of the improved irrigation infrastructure would be transferred gradually to the WUAs. To ensure that a scheme management code and training, will need to be put in place for the Government irrigation structures (DoWUs) and the
- Operation and Maintenance Manual
- Water allocation modalities for the targeted schemes

Future management of the upgraded system will be put in the hands of the WUAs.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Security

#### Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Governance | High | Medium

**Description**

High security threat inhibits travel especially of UN staff and contractors who are subject to very stringent security requirements. The security measures that the international staff must abide by make movements difficult and increase the cost of travel. Security threats also result in increased project management costs compared to projects with more secure operating environments and are estimated at 10% of total project costs.

#### Mitigation Measures

To the United Nations Security Management System (UNSMS) enables the activities of UN organizations across acceptable levels of risk. The UNSMS is a comprehensive framework for managing security risks in accordance with the policies of the United Nations. At country level, Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) have been developed detailing requirements and procedures on UN official travel and international staff travel. These SOPs are reviewed by the UN Security Management Unit (SMU). Designated Area of Risk is divided into seven SRM areas with varying security risk levels. All UN personnel are required to complete a Security Risk Assessment (SRA) prior to deployment. The SMU also includes FAO. The SMU and its mitigation measures take into consideration all possible risks and TA personnel attach themselves to the relevant SRM area. This is achieved by having a majority of local staff, or of public sector extension agents, competitively procuring service providers through competitive tendering, and ensuring that the project has a strong field presence such as FTS, field days and working with CWW. In addition, to further mitigate risks and to ensure the safety of UN personnel, the SMU will also provide training for staff in the relevant SRM area. The SMU will also facilitate extension staff from the relevant ministries. To this end, staff at the MAWR and the MoA will also facilitate and supervise the implementation of the project. In addition, the SMU will also ensure that all UN personnel are provided with local transport and accommodation as required by the SRM area. The SMU will also ensure that all UN personnel are provided with local transport and accommodation as required by the SRM area.

Governators which have easy access to the SRM areas will be subject to fewer stringent travel security restrictions than those located in difficult territories like Iraq and other countries to ensure that capacity of funded staff and service providers is not compromised. The SMU will also ensure that all UN personnel are provided with local transport and accommodation as required by the SRM area.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Governance

#### Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Governance | High | Medium

**Description**


---
**Page 80**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 75

Iraq has been identified as a country with extreme fragility by most international development organizations around the world. This is the midst of a political, security and socio-economic transition. The overall constraints relate to unstable governance, corruption, ethnic tensions and conflict, economic depression, financial crises, etc., Iraq is a typical example of a fragile state.

The volatile situation and the associated security risks are not very conducive for private sector investment either and impact on the ability of the government to deliver public services and provide basic social services to its people. The security situation on the ground is unstable and becoming increasingly fraught, with the emergence of new and more sophisticated threats. Iraq has been identified as a country at high risk of state failure and political disaffiliation (EU, 2013). Fundamental political and economic reforms are required to achieve any meaningful form of unity in this deeply divided society. The government is also facing significant challenges in terms of its ability to build the national capacity to deal with development and economic issues, service delivery, poverty alleviation and climate risk threats.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The different development agencies working on the ground have learnt to navigate the security and instability and dealt with it through a combination of mitigation measures. The focus has been on building resilience in communities and by not taking undue risks. The project’s focus is on two critical areas of need, mainly water and energy is expected to mobilize the people and build their capacity for self-reliance.

The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Iraqi government and other stakeholders. The mobilization of people on issues of common interest is expected to enhance collaboration and cooperation. The outcomes are expected to be sustainable as they will be embedded within the social fabric of the population, with the dominant ethnic sectarian parties remaining in control.

## Selected Risk(s) of Post-Conflict Reconstruction

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Iraq has historically not had a good governance and weak capacity because of political and administrative weaknesses, corruption, ethnic tensions and conflict, economic depression, financial crises, etc., Iraq is a typical example of a fragile state. The country’s security situation on the ground is unstable and becoming increasingly fraught with the emergence of new and more sophisticated threats. Iraq has been identified as a country at high risk of state failure and political disaffiliation (EU, 2013). Fundamental political and economic reforms are required to achieve any meaningful form of unity in this deeply divided society. The government is also facing significant challenges in terms of its ability to build the national capacity to deal with development and economic issues, service delivery, poverty alleviation and climate risk threats. |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Experience of several external agencies have shown that it is possible nevertheless to work in post conflict countries by building a strong relationship between the project team and the host government and convening power of external agencies like the UN. The presence of a third-party entity to facilitate project management has proved to be an effective mechanism for ensuring that the project is implemented effectively, efficiently and transparently. This will strengthen implementation and institutional memory. Inadequate local financial management and procurement capacity is best addressed by building on the lessons learned from other projects in Iraq where this has been successfully done. Technical capacity and administrative ability has been proven and well established in Iraq and will be used for the project.

The presence of a strong third-party entity to facilitate project management has proved to be an effective mechanism such as through a strong Project Steering Committee which can facilitate and expedite decision-making and ensure that the project is implemented effectively, efficiently and transparently. This will strengthen implementation and institutional memory. Inadequate local financial management and procurement capacity is best addressed by building on the lessons learned from other projects in Iraq where this has been successfully done.

## Selected Risk(s) of Implementation Delays and Cost Overruns

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Approved requests at the level of both FAO and the project are usually delayed project implementation and can, in turn, lead to cost overruns due to rising prices. In the recent past countries have also experienced exchange rate instability due to the impact fuel and energy prices. These delays and increase in cost may adversely affect the economic returns from the project. |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

FAO has a strong team in place at the country office that is able to work with the Government to ensure that all approvals are provided as soon as possible. In addition, FAO will put in place a strong team for ensuring that procurement is undertaken in time and that there is no delay in the release of funds from the donor. This will help to ensure that it is implemented well in time. Cost over runs can partially be met through exchange rate gains in case of delays.

Selected Risk Factor 5: Financial Aspects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Low | Low | Low |

Description

The country's sovereign risk rating was assessed as CC by the OECD’s Economic Intelligence Unit which is described as “somewhat weak capacity and commitment to honour obligations, a patchy record of transparency and steadfastness to be in default on some obligations (ESU 2021). The currency has a CCR rating which entails a questionable capacity and commitment to honour obligations and payments. The government’s revenue generation capacity is also considered weak with the country having a high level of public debt as a share of GDP. This is reflected by the low credit rating in the downgrade. Efforts to improve commercial banks’ ability to meet capital requirements will continue, but political inertia will hamper banking sector reforms. Access to financial markets for government securities will remain a core weakness.

(Mitigation Measures)

The project is designed in such a manner as so not to put any additional financial burden on the Government of Iraq and to ensure that the project does not have an adverse impact on the country’s public finances. The project will be funded by employee salaries and retiree salaries and an almost complete halt in government public investment. The private sector is not expected to contribute significantly, as it has been unable to access credit from commercial banks due to the weak credit rating. In addition, there are no investments from the financial sector for credit funds to small holders during the project implementation. The Government is committed to paying salaries and pensions of civil servants and public employees in full and on time. However, under Component 1 (estimated at around USD 228,000 m), it is expected that the Government’s revenue generation capacity will be insufficient to meet its obligations for this component. This is due to the fact that the government has a high level of public accounts, reducing the risk that the sovereign will be unable to service its debt. However, a substantial decline in foreign exchange earnings and an increase in domestic arrears could lead to a further deterioration in the country’s credit rating. The Central Bank of Iraq deviated the draw-in pay to USD dollar at ID 1.450/USD1 from ID1.182/USD1 previously. Currency devaluation has also been observed since 2019. In addition, the government is expected to receive a total of USD 3 billion BIP package estimated to be worth USD 6 bn, with funds to be disbursed over three years. A return to a trade surplus in 2021 and beyond will help to reduce the current account deficit.

Selected Risk Factor 6: Technical and operational

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | Medium | Medium |

Description

Projects have been very slow at getting off the ground, especially where public sector ministries are in charge of executing projects and implementing on the ground. However, some agencies such as the World Bank and JICA have made progress on the ground. Others like the International Fund for Agriculture Development (IFAD) have had very limited operations on the ground and have not been able to complete the disbursement requirements after more than two or three years of project approval.

(Mitigation Measures)

The experience of some agencies like the World Bank and JICA provides good lessons on how to expedite performance on the ground. The World Bank has been able to build a strong active portfolio in the country, which has resulted in important initiatives, notably in sectoral emergency response, water supply and sanitation, and urban development. The World Bank has also been able to mobilize USD 1 billion for disaster risk protection system among others, and private sector investments of more than USD 3 billion. The Bank has also been able to provide USD 20 million for the establishment of a national climate change center. In order to ensure successful implementation of this project, some lessons will be emulated such as the importance of national ownership, building the capacity of public institutions and ensuring that the project is well integrated into existing systems. The World Bank has been able to build strong credibility in the country and enjoys the confidence of key Government Agencies. FAO has on several occasions provided technical assistance for projects implemented by other agencies. It will be an important aspect of design to ensure quick start-up and implementation on the ground is the management of the PRMU at FAO. The PRMU will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in a timely manner, and service companies or local NGOs will be hired through an LDA for several aspects of implementation such as the CWII.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 77

## Description

The capacity of the MoA and MoWR and other government institutions to provide services to the agriculture sector has drastically deteriorated over the past 20 years. Budget cuts reduced the level of services resulting in the departure of skilled personnel, particularly in the areas of research, extension, and disease control. The recovery of the capacity of these services is very slow, and there is a need for solid investment in human resources to restore this capacity. In addition, many of the government institutions' research assets (buildings, labs and farms) have been damaged. Technicians are not sufficient in number or quality. The sanctions imposed by the international community on Iran have also had an adverse effect on the country's agriculture sector. There is a lack of a well extension and training strategy with adequate operating budget, involving effective decentralization; there is no clear policy for the involvement of private sector institutions in providing services to women and youth. Finally, coordination among ministries is poor.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project does not depend on the implementation of any specific measure by government directly and will contract in qualified service providers for the purpose. However, for long-term sustainability and up-scaling technical assistance will be provided to build the capacity of the public sector agencies. In addition, the project will also try a large number of extension staff at the governorate level to increase their capacity. The project will also provide training on the use of modern irrigation systems, including drip irrigation efficiency, organize smallholders into farmer field schools and for field days. In addition, service providers or local institutions will be involved in providing services to women and youth. The overall impact of the different measures is expected to mitigate some of the weak institutional capacity of the extension agencies on the ground.

### Selected Risk Factor: E. Farmer Organizations

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

The authority of traditional Farmer Organizations (WUAs, Cooperatives, farmer associations, etc.) has been eroded by the unrest of the last two decades. In addition, various efforts at reform have fragmented landholding and ownership structures. Support is needed to ensure that these groups regain their role and contribute to the development of the irrigation systems and agricultural extension services in a sustainable manner.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

There are several existing examples of successful collaboration between the Ministry of Agriculture with other institutions such as the National Water Company, the National Research Institute of Agriculture, and the University of Tehran. These include: (i) the national programme for the use of on-farm modern irrigation systems; (ii) the national programme for the establishment of a national agricultural meteorological network; (iii) the programme for the genetic improvement of rice varieties; (iv) the programme for the establishment of an early warning system for monitoring and mitigation of climate change risks, which will support the country in identifying needed resources to address these risks. The project will build on this experience by providing technical assistance to farmers and their formal and informal organizations to ensure that the project activities do not increase the current perception of disputes and conflicts between communities and stakeholders.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and Social Safeguards (max. 750 words, approximately 1.4 pages)

### The project is in accordance with FAO and GCF's Environmental and Social Policy (ESSP), the SRAVII! project design underwent an environmental and social assessment following FAO's environmental and social safeguards guidance (FAO, 2015). An Action Plan was developed to address identified risks and impacts. The ESSP is a living document that will be updated as new information becomes available or as the project evolves during its implementation phase. The ESSP includes a list of actions to trigger for the project, screening criteria for activities, environmental and social impacts of the activities, and measures and mitigation strategies. These actions are in line with FAO and GCF ESS Policy, and national legislation, and adhere to whichever is more stringent.

### During these stakeholder consultations, the Grievance Redress Mechanism will also be presented and explained. Consultation meetings will be held with the following stakeholders: Government of Ethiopia (GoE), Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), Water Resources and Buildings (AWRB). The AWRB will be presented by the PMU in the Project Steering Committee for approval. In order to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities, the GoE will be involved at all stages of the project design process including environmental and social safeguards processes (including GIM), interacting on a regular basis with key stakeholders and being kept informed.

### The consultations held in the frame of workshops and multistakeholder and bilateral meetings at the national, government, regional, woreda, and kebele levels will be used to identify the project's environmental and social safeguards components. These aspects of the initiative. Furthermore, this process would ensure that the design met national priorities (irrigation efficiency improvement, water resource management, climate change adaptation, food security, etc.), addressed the needs of the target project areas, and main stakeholders.

### The components include implementing climate-resilient agronomic systems and practices, improving access to markets for smallholder farmers, increasing women's participation in agricultural activities, strengthening communities, more efficient use of irrigation water, and the development of renewable energy infrastructure. A key risk faced by the project is that it may not be able to address all identified risks and impacts due to limited resources.

### **ESS 1 (natural resource management):** Risks are related to infrastructure works on irrigation canals and installation of solar panels. The project will ensure that the construction activities do not cause any negative environmental impact or damage to the environment, and that the waste generated from these activities is disposed of at an appropriate site and in an appropriate manner. The execution of works by contractors will comply with established safety standards and procedures.

### **ESS 2 (plant genetic resources for food and agriculture):** Salt and drought-tolerant crop varieties that will be determined during FTS are varieties already commonly used in the country, developed and tested by the National Agricultural Research System (NARS), and have been approved by the Ministry of Agriculture. The project will not introduce seed variety. No seeds will be procured and no new planting material (crop varieties) will be introduced into the country.

### **ESS 7 (gender work):** ESS 7 (gender equality) Potential risks could be related to equitable participation in project activities, including access to resources, decision-making power, and benefits from project outputs. Climate change action will address these issues for protection against COVID-19. Women's participation is ensured through providing training and protective measures and gender mainstreaming.

### The project is designed to ensure benefits to all target groups and people that will be impacted by project activities. During the inception phase, the project will develop an Indigenous Peoples Plan, as per GCF IPs Policy, in case of presence of indigenous peoples in the project area. The project will also provide a Gender Action Plan (GAP) during the inception phase.

### **Action 143:** The project will identify and mitigate SEAH risks or potential adverse impacts on women, men, girls and boys as early as possible as a part of environmental and social risk screening and reflect such risks or impacts in relevant safeguards.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### 144.
The project design is based on the understanding that gender dynamics affect resilience and the capacity to adapt to climate change, with women being disproportionately impacted by climate change (Annex E). The project aims to empower rural women in the three target Governments through giving them visibility as key stakeholders in decision-making processes. This will be achieved by providing training for women on climate change practices, providing opportunities for decision-making and leadership. The Gender Action Plan of the project ensures that nearly 100% of the project’s budget is allocated to gender equality and women empowerment. Women are also more vulnerable to climate change through their increased vulnerability and active engagement in water management and leadership roles in climate change adaptation.

In Component 1, the GAP focuses on increasing women's visibility in the irrigation and water management sector by (i) addressing women strategic positions through undertaking a study documenting women's roles in short- and long-term planning and decision-making processes; (ii) building capacity for women in the water sector at all levels of their direct representation in government bodies such as the WUGAs, (iii) building capacity for women in the water sector at all levels of their direct representation in government bodies such as the WUGAs, (iv) building capacity for women in the water sector at all levels of their direct representation in government bodies such as the WUGAs, and (v) building capacity for women in the water sector at all levels of their direct representation in government bodies such as the WUGAs.

In Component 2, the GAP ensures that rural women's role as agents of change for climate resilience is made visible and recognized. This will be achieved by (i) providing rural women with access to information on climate adaptation through gender-sensitive ICT4C; (ii) ensuring that rural women have the skills and knowledge needed to adapt their livelihoods in response to climate change, including access to information about climate change impacts and adaptation strategies; (iii) building capacity for rural women's participation in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation at all levels of government; (iv) providing rural women with access to financial resources necessary for implementing climate-resilient practices on their farms or other landholdings, including access to credit and microfinance services; (v) ensuring that rural women have the skills and knowledge needed to adapt their livelihoods in response to climate change, including access to information about climate change impacts and adaptation strategies.

This will be achieved by (i) ensuring that rural women have access to information on climate adaptation through gender-sensitive ICT4C; (ii) building capacity for rural women's participation in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation at all levels of government; (iii) providing rural women with access to financial resources necessary for implementing climate-resilient practices on their farms or other landholdings, including access to credit and microfinance services; (iv) ensuring that rural women have the skills and knowledge needed to adapt their livelihoods in response to climate change

### G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

#### *No confidential information.* The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed fully by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
#### *With confidential information.* The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed to any third party without prior written consent of the accredited entity. The funding proposal will be submitted providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
- A copy for internal use by the Secretariat and the GCF project supervision team.
- A redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled "Strengthening Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agriculture Livelihoods in Iraq" in the Republic of Iraq as included in the funding proposal submitted by FAO to us on April 6/2022.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Environment, the National Environment Commission and the Republic of Iraq.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

1. The government of the Republic of Iraq has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
2. The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of the Republic of Iraq;
3. In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP249

Proposal name: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agriculture Livelihoods in Iraq (SRWALI)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

Country: Republic of Iraq

Project/Programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- Addresses critical climate vulnerabilities in Iraq’s agriculture sector, focusing on water scarcity, irrigation efficiency and climate-resilient farming practices, with large reach and impact on 1 million women beneficiaries.
- Comprehensive approach combining infrastructure investments, capacity-building and policy reforms to enhance agricultural and energy sectors to create market opportunities for private sector engagement. The adoption of Information Communication Technology for Climate Change Adaptation and Early Warning Systems can stimulate the growth of technology markets based on agriculture and climate resilience.
- Strong economic rationale: 16 per cent economic returns and USD 474 million net present value.

#### Points of caution
- Security and governance challenges in Iraq could impact implementation.
- Policy/regulatory reforms needed to enable and sustain changes.
- Risks related to future identification of Indigenous Peoples.


---
**Page 101**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

1. Iraq faces significant threats from climate change, particularly impacting its agriculture sector. The project aims to build long-term resilience of Vulnerable Agriculture Livelihoods in Iraq (SVALLI) project proposed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) joint with the Government of Iraq. The project will promote water resources conservation through integrated and sustainable approaches.
2. The overall objective of this project is to build long-term resilience in the agriculture sector, focusing on the most vulnerable communities and promoting sustainability.

### Project cost

1. Iraq’s agriculture sector is a crucial part of its economy and food security but it is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased temperatures, altered precipitation patterns, and more frequent extreme weather events that can devastate crops, water resources, and the livelihoods of millions of people reliant on agriculture.
2. The project aims to benefit 1,044,800 people directly and 913,334 people indirectly. Those in need include smallholder farmers and rural communities who are most at risk from the adverse effects of climate change.

### Environmental and social safeguards (ESG) category for this project is B

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Strengthening resilience against climate-induced water scarcity (Total cost: USD 27.05 million; GEF cost: USD 31.22 million in grants)

- **Component 1 aims to tackle water scarcity and variability exacerbated by climate change through the development of new infrastructure and renewable energy systems.
- The project focuses on irrigation efficiency and water optimization, avoiding technological solutions that are not sustainable or appropriate for smallholder crop-based systems. This option appeared to be the most efficient and effective in dealing with climate change impacts.

#### Component 2: Climate-smart agriculture production (Total cost: USD 78.01 million; GEF cost: USD 31.11 million in grants)

- **This project component addresses barriers faced by farmers in adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices, such as a lack of awareness about efficient irrigation, crop water requirements and appropriate crop mixes, compounded by limited knowledge of improved market opportunities.
- To address these issues, the project will upgrade the skills of extension agents and farmers by providing training, organizing local farmer field schools, and establishing networks for ongoing interaction with the private sector. The Farmer Field School methodology, deemed the most efficient for implementing


---
**Page 102**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

The project aims to significantly enhance climate resilience in Iraq's agriculture sector, directly benefiting 1,048,800 people.

- By focusing on drought-tolerant agricultural practices, improved water management and infrastructure development, the project targets the most vulnerable communities. These interventions aim to increase agricultural productivity while ensuring food security and enhanced livelihoods, which are critical for economic and social stability of Iraq.
  
- Increased agricultural productivity will be achieved through the adoption of climate-resilient practices such as the use of drought-resistant crop varieties and improved soil management techniques. This approach ensures that farmers can continue to produce crops even when faced with changing climatic conditions. Improved food security will come from both increased productivity and the development of storage facilities to reduce post-harvest losses, thereby ensuring a more stable supply chain.

- Enhanced livelihoods are another key outcome, as the project provides training and resources for smallholder farmers to increase their income generation capacity. By directly benefiting 1,048,800 people and indirectly supporting many more, the project represents a significant investment in Iraq's future. It addresses multiple dimensions of climate resilience, from individual farmer-level policy to creating a comprehensive framework for sustainable agricultural development.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: N/A


---
**Page 103**
---

3 Sustainable development potential Score: N/A

The project contributes to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):

1. SDG2 (Zero hunger) and SDG15 (Life on land): By enhancing food security, improving livelihoods and reducing climate vulnerability in Iraq's agriculture sector.
2. SDG8 (Decent work and economic growth): The project focuses on sustainable agricultural practices that increase productivity while maintaining soil health, addressing the consequences of food insecurity and climate vulnerability, providing long-term solutions that benefit both people and the environment.

The project's contribution to SDG2 is particularly noteworthy; by increasing agricultural productivity and reducing post-harvest losses, the project helps ensure a stable and sufficient food supply. However, achieving this goal remains a significant challenge. The project's emphasis on training and capacity-building also empowers farmers, equipping them with the knowledge and skills needed to maintain and improve their livelihoods.

In addition to SDG2, the project supports SDG13 by promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and increase carbon sequestration. These practices help communities to adapt to the impacts of climate change, reducing their vulnerability and increasing resilience. The project also strengthens institutional frameworks for climate action, ensuring that climate resilience is integrated into national development plans and strategies.

In particular, the project supports other goals such as SDG6 (Clean water and sanitation) and SDG15 (Life on land). Improved water management systems enhance water availability and quality, supporting sustainable agricultural practices. These include soil conservation and agroforestry, helping to preserve ecosystems and biodiversity, contributing to the overall health and sustainability of the environment.


---
**Page 104**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: N/A

25. Iraq’s agriculture sector is highly vulnerable to climate change with limited resources to adapt. The project addresses critical gaps in capacity, infrastructure and policy support, providing much-needed support to vulnerable communities. Focusing on the most vulnerable communities ensures that the benefits are equitably distributed, targeting those who need it most.

26. One of the key needs addressed by the project is the lack of climate-resilient infrastructure. The construction of water harvesting structures, irrigation systems and storage facilities provides a reliable supply of water for agricultural activities in the region. These interventions help to ensure a reliable water supply, reduce post-harvest losses and improve overall productivity, thereby enhancing food security and improving livelihoods for vulnerable communities.

27. Capacity building is another critical need addressed by the project. Many farmers lack the knowledge and skills needed to adopt climate-resilient practices. The project’s training programmes and technical assistance provide farmers with the tools they need to improve their practices and adapt to changing climatic conditions. As a result, the benefits of the project are sustainable as farmers are empowered to continue improving their practices even after the project ends.

28. Policy support is also a key component of the project's approach. By developing and updating climate risk-informed agricultural policies, the project helps to create an enabling environment for climate-resilient agriculture in Iraq. This ensures that the benefits of the project are integrated into national development plans and strategies, enhancing the overall resilience of Iraq’s agriculture sector.

3.5 Country ownership

Scale: N/A

29. The SWAII project aligns with Iraq's national climate strategies and priorities, demonstrating strong country ownership. The project has received substantial support from the government and key stakeholders, ensuring effective implementation and sustainability. This alignment with national priorities ensures that the project is relevant and addresses the most pressing needs of Iraqi farmers.

30. The involvement of local communities and institutions in project design and execution further enhances country ownership. Through participatory approaches during the planning and implementation process, the project fosters a sense of ownership and commitment. This participatory approach ensures that the interventions are tailored to local needs and conditions, increasing their effectiveness and sustainability.

31. The project's alignment with national climate strategies and priorities is evident in its focus on capacity building and policy support. These components not only strengthen institutional capacity-building but also align with national efforts to strengthen institutional frameworks for climate action. This alignment ensures that the benefits of the project are integrated into broader national development plans, enhancing the overall resilience of Iraq's agriculture sector.

32. The strong commitment from the government and key stakeholders is also reflected in the project’s implementation arrangements. FAO, as an AE, has a strong track record of working with the government and local institutions in Iraq. This partnership ensures that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently, contributing to the overall resilience of Iraq's agriculture sector.

33. No-objection letters have been issued by the country, confirming this support.


---
**Page 105**
---

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Score: N/A**

The SRVALLI project presents a cost-effective approach to building climate resilience, with substantial co-benefits for rural development. The project's design includes a combination of infrastructure development, capacity-building and policy support, ensuring a holistic and integrated approach to climate resilience. This comprehensive approach maximizes the impact of the investments on both economic growth and social welfare.

The economic internal rate of return for the project is favourable, indicating strong economic viability. The project’s interventions are designed to generate substantial economic benefits, including increased agricultural productivity, improved food security, and enhanced livelihoods. These benefits contribute to the overall economic stability and development of Iraq, justifying the investment in climate resilience.

The project's design also includes mechanisms for adaptive management, allowing for adjustments based on monitoring and feedback. This flexibility ensures that the project remains effective over time as conditions change. The monitoring and evaluation framework included in the project design ensures that progress and outcomes are tracked, and lessons learned are applied to future interventions.

Efficiency and effectiveness are further enhanced by the project's participatory approach, which ensures that interventions are tailored to local needs and conditions. By engaging with stakeholders at all levels of decision-making, the project demonstrates that the interventions are relevant, effective and sustainable. This participatory approach also fosters a sense of ownership and commitment among stakeholders, increasing their support for the project and its objectives.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Project overview:** The project aims to enhance the climate resilience of rural households by supporting climate adaptive infrastructure (such as improvement of irrigation canals), promotion of technologies and practices (such as use of salt and drought-tolerant crop varieties, improved water management techniques) that are expected to increase agricultural yields of farming households. The project's environmental and social co-benefits include better land use management through nature-based solutions such as the use of shelterbelts, reversing desertification processes; reduced soil erosion and sedimentation; increased use of organic fertilizers and pesticides. The use of over-canal solar PV arrays brings about on-costs benefits to farmers by reducing water losses from evaporation and increasing water availability potentially mitigating growth of aquatic weeds. Among the social co-benefits include better health due to improved sanitation, reduced exposure to hazardous chemicals and increased access to safe drinking water distribution for end users.

**Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) risk category and safeguard instrument:** The AE has classified this project as Category 3. The project will involve upgrading of existing irrigation canals, installation of canal-fog PV systems, capacity building, extension program on climate-resilient agriculture practices and policy support. The environmental and social impacts are mostly linked to infrastructure works. These impacts are localized, reversible and mitigable through the implementation of mitigation measures as described in the ESMP. The Secretariat agrees with the categorization, and it is within the AE's accreditation level. The AE has prepared an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). Although the sites for the irrigation canal upgrading and solar PV installation have been identified, the detailed


---
**Page 106**
---

### Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The project implementation will only be undertaken after discussion with stakeholders.

#### 40. Compliance with GCF's ESS Standards

##### 40.1. Identification of Environmental, Social Risks and Impacts

The ESMS provides a general E&S assessment for the project, identifying triggers of E&S risks, potential risks, and impacts, and corresponding mitigation measures, and setting up an environmental and social monitoring system.

##### 40.2. Project-Specific Environmental and Social Risk Assessment

The ESMS includes the application and adaptation of the GCF’s E&S screening checklist to determine risk categories for each site-specific activity. The project will be subjecting each site-specific activity to the AE’s E&S screening checklist to determine risk category, and implementing appropriate mitigation measures as required.

##### 40.3. Labor and Working Conditions

Labor and working conditions standards are addressed under the AES ES 04 (Decent Work) which the AE has flagged, noting that the project will generate employment and that the project operates in areas with high occurrences of violence against women and girls. The project will ensure that all workers identified under this standard, measures such as encouraging the use of local labor inputs at each site, ensuring that all workers are paid according to the national minimum wage for construction sites by applying age verification before the employment of workers is proposed.

##### 40.3.1. General Requirements

In terms of working conditions, the project will ensure compliance with general rules and regulations on OHS (ii). Ensure workers are equipped with protective gear (e.g., helmets, gloves) as required for their work tasks. Workers must be provided with information at work sites and necessary information on rescue during emergency; (iv) Ensure workers are trained on OH&S risk prevention and management on site.

##### 40.3.2. Specific Measures

Another measure is the adoption of guidance on prevention of communicable diseases such as COVID-19, an necessary. A further measure is to ensure that all workers have access to safe drinking water in the project construction area, to address potential gender-based and ethnicity-based (minorities) discrimination in terms of access to water.

##### 40.3.3. Prohibition on Exploitation

The project will also prohibit any form of exploitation or abuse of rights of workers hired through the project. If risks are deemed significant, management measures at the project level may be required and should include a review of the Project Implementation Procedure and/or an OHS Plan. Labor and working conditions involving harmful, exploitative, discriminatory or compulsory practices, including child labor or significant occupational health and safety risks are also excluded from funding under this project.

#### 43. Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

In terms of resource efficiency, the project will use solar panels to generate electricity for the cooling system, thereby minimizing evaporation and the use of pre-piped water metering. The ESMS has identified potential pollution during construction period (primarily in the form of increased sedimentation) and operational period (primarily in the form of increased emissions). Measures are proposed to prevent or mitigate these potential impacts. During operation, the solar panels will contribute to displaced power capacity from fossil fuel power plants. The covering/shading of algae has also been identified as a measure that can be taken during construction to reduce evaporation and lead to low evaporation. The shading also reduces the growth of algae. The project will not lead to


---
**Page 107**
---

### ESS4: Community Health, Safety and Security

Construction works and maintenance activities of solar panels on canals and construction and maintenance of buried irrigation pipes will have to be carried out in a manner that does not affect the security of residents in the project communities.

### ESS5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The project will not require or result in involuntary resettlement or physical displacement. The ESRS reports that one of the critical issues for the project is land acquisition, resettlement, or relocation. The upgraded infrastructure (irrigation canals) will be constructed on existing agricultural lands. However, as per the Non-Eligibility List, the following activities are not eligible for funding under the project: a. relocation and/or compensation of households or businesses; b. use of the project to provide land and/or a tool to support and/or enable resettlement of local people and village consolidation; c. land acquisition using eminent domain powers; d. construction of new settlements or expansion of existing settlements.

### ESS6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources

The project will not have adverse effects on Iraq's biodiversity, emphasizing its richness and the severe stress it has endured due to conflicts and various projects on agricultural lands. The ESRS notes that the project will not affect any protected areas since the canals to be upgraded are within existing agricultural areas. Hence, it does not foresee or cause any negative impacts on biodiversity. However, as per the Non-Eligibility List (NEL), the project will have potential for inadvertent introduction of invasive species into the farms, the project will avoid such activities and ensure that all farm inputs are sourced from local suppliers reflected in the Non-Eligibility List which explicitly exclude from funding sub-activities that involve "introduction of exotic species where they are already present in the vicinity or known from similar settings to have adverse" and "acquisition of land for new settlements and existing or proposed protected areas."

### ESS7: Indigenous Peoples

The project does not contain any Indigenous Peoples, nor will it be affected by the project's activities.

### ESS8: Cultural Heritage

The project will avoid known sites by requiring that the selection of canals for upgrade, and layout of the selected distributary canals and their watercourses, avoids areas with potential adverse effects on cultural heritage or physical cultural resources. The Non-Eligibility List also specifies the exclusion from funding any activity that would damage or result to a loss in cultural property, including sites having archaeological (prehistoric), paleontological, historical, religious, cultural, and unique natural values. In case of

### Implementation Arrangements

An Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Specialist will be hired within the Project Management Unit (PMU) for the project’s duration. The ESS Specialist will work closely with the PMU to ensure that the project complies with all relevant national laws, regulations, and policies; incorporate feedback, and ensure stakeholders’ capacity to implement ESMF through capacity building activities. The ESS Specialist will also be responsible for managing grievance mechanisms, manage grievance trends, and oversee sub-activity screening and ESMF implementation. The PMU, with relevant government counterparts, will be responsible for the overall management of the project activities and will track performance at national and Governmental levels. Executing entities will be required to collect and submit reports to the PMU, which will subsequently prepare progress reports.

#### Stakeholder engagement

The project was developed with the involvement of key stakeholders through a multi-stakeholder process that included three national-level workshops with key stakeholders. The project also held meetings with key local stakeholders, including WUSA members to discuss key activities for project involvement. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) will be prepared and updated as necessary based on information so far conducted and set out the plan for further stakeholder engagements during the project implementation period.

#### Implementation

Implementation would consist of Annual Work Plan and Budget (AWPB) consultations both at the country level with the Government counterparts and at the regional level. The PMU will review the work undertaken in the previous fiscal year, assess if activities are on track, validate results, or identify, where necessary, any modifications that need to be made during implementation.

#### Grievance redress mechanism (GRM)

The ESMF has outlined a grievance redress mechanism (GRM) for project participants and other stakeholders. The GRM addresses complaints. Contact information and instructions for filing complaints will be provided during all project-related activities, including the signing of contracts and agreements with contractors.

The Project Management Unit (PMU) will manage grievances related to environmental and social standards, with the PMU Project Coordinator responsible for documenting and reporting on received grievances. The PMU will also ensure that any information or data collected is transmitted to the FAO Iraq Representation, the FAO Regional Office for Near East and North Africa, and ultimately to the Director of the Inspector General. All interactions and resolutions will be thoroughly documented.

#### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The SEAH policy is a key component of the ESMF. The policy complies with SEAH provisions of the GCF Revolving Environmental and Social Policy. The ESMF has identified that there are significant risks associated with the environmental and social nature of the project and its implementation. Given the high contextual SEAH risks present in the host country, the PMU will ensure that all project activities are conducted in a manner where SEAH risks are identified, assessed, prevented, and managed throughout the project.

The ESMF considers that there is a significant risk of sexual exploitation, abuse, or harassment (SEAH) specific risks, such as the lack of a strong legal system to enforce laws and low levels of prosecution for SEAH in the project areas and the high rates of illiteracy and limited ability of SSI practitioners. The PMU will ensure that all project activities are conducted in a manner where SEAH risks are identified, assessed, prevented, and managed throughout the project.

The AEK acknowledges the potential for women’s participation or employment in the project may exacerbate existing gender-based violence (GBV) risks. To mitigate these risks, the PMU will use an SEAH risk matrix and explicitly outline safeguarding measures, such as working with local officials to ensure that all project activities are conducted in a manner where SEAH risks are identified, assessed, prevented, and managed throughout the project.

The PMU will work closely with government stakeholders, identifying champions, supporters, and change-makers within the government on SEAH, conducting SEAH awareness-raising and sensitization campaigns in the


---
**Page 109**
