2. Gender policy

The AIF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore meets the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The assessment provides a detailed overview of the socioeconomic situation of rural women in Iraq, their political participation, and their access to resources. It also examines the impact of climate change on gender-based violence, the impact of the coronavirus disease 2019 pandemic, gender norms, and other related issues. Based on this assessment, the gender action plan outlines specific activities aimed at addressing the gender gaps identified in the assessment and that are relevant to the project.

The gender action plan outlines three main components: (i) improving access for women to resources and livelihoods of vulnerable individuals, communities and regions from a gender perspective; (ii) the gender action plan outlines strategies to address gender-related constraints. The AIF will implement these strategies through several mechanisms that include training on gender issues and the development of gender-responsive training modules to address gender-based violence in rural areas. These training modules will be used for capacity building in agricultural production and water-saving technologies. In addition, the project will build women's capacity; it has outlined a number of activities aimed at improving access to resources and livelihoods of vulnerable individuals, communities and regions from a gender perspective.

### 4.3

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

56. The project aims to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural households in Iraq's rural communities across Najaf, Karbala, and Muthanna. The project will enhance climate resilience by improving water management practices, increasing access to irrigation infrastructure, and developing policies for improved water management and climate adaptation technologies. Total grant co-financing is USD 10 million from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), USD 238 million from FAO, and USD 79 million from the Government of Iraq in the form of a grant and in-kind contributions, for a co-financing ratio of 1:5:3.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (low risk)

57. FAO serves as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project in Iraq, overseeing its execution through FAO-Iraq and collaborating with four ministries (Environment, Water Resources, Agriculture, and Planning). The AE has a strong track record of implementing projects in Iraq, including its successful in-kind co-financing. FAO signs subsidiary agreements with them to delineate roles and responsibilities for the project's implementation.

58. FAO ensures effective implementation, drawing from its extensive track record in similar contexts. The AE is well-positioned to execute this project due to its strong track record and FAO’s capacity to implement the project and navigate challenges, ensuring compliance with Iraq’s laws and regulations. This builds on the AE's role as the GCF funding confidence from the Secretariat of the AE and its EEs' capacity to execute the project activities.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

59. Security risk: The SSIWAL project acknowledges the significant security risks associated with its implementation in Iraq, given the volatile security environment and regional instability. While the focus on rural areas such as Najaf, Karbala, and Muthanna helps mitigate some of these risks due to their lower population density compared to urban centers, the risk remains a concern.

60. To address these risks, FAO outlines several effective mitigation strategies: prioritizing local staff, establishing strong relationships with local authorities, improving security practices, and strategic local partnerships to enhance situational awareness. Prioritizing local staffing and collaborating with local NGOs addressing emphasis areas with a history of stability and established community networks are key measures.

61. These proactive measures are crucial for navigating Iraq's complex security context and ensuring the project's successful implementation in these regions.

#### 4.3.4 Co-financing

62. We acknowledge the stable co-financing totaling USD 7,000,000 (USD 682 million from grants to FAO, USD 1,830 million in kind from FAO, and USD 2,800 million in kind from Iraq). This co-financing is provided by the Government of Iraq (Executing Entity), resulting in a co-financing ratio of 1:5:34. If co-financing does not materialize, this may impede project implementation. The GCF grant and FAO's in-kind contributions are considered as part of the co-financing status in the term sheet/FAA are considered adequate mitigation measures.

#### 4.3.5 Project viability (and concessionality)

63. The use of grants (%100 percent concessionality) is expected to be used for project implementation, with no compounded economic and environmental challenges. The proposal further reflects that Iraq has faced significant financial constraints in recent years due to the COVID-19 pandemic and other factors that have significantly strained public resources. Moreover, the agriculture sector, which is crucial for employment and food security, is severely impacted by climate change, particularly water

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

The compliance risk assessment is rated as medium risk, primarily because of concerns surrounding money-laundering and financing terrorism. Despite the rigorous FATF internal controls and enhanced due diligence measures implemented by Iraq, including biannual cash log updates, the inherent risks associated with financial activities in complex environments like Iraq remain. The dynamic nature of financial crime risks necessitates continuous vigilance and adaptive management to uphold the integrity of the project.

## Results monitoring and reporting

The project’s theory of change showcases the transformational pathway through which the project will generate adaptation and mitigation impact while contributing to important co-benefits. Two of the project outcomes focus on providing direct benefits to the vulnerable populations in Iraq, while the third outcome complements creating an enabling environment for national stakeholders to drive long-term climate resilience. The logical framework includes a set of specific indicators that reflect critical assumptions noted in the theory of change relating to post-implementation operational capacity and infrastructure.

The logical framework has identified GCF indicators applicable to measure the results from the project’s outcomes and outputs. As the project covers multiple mitigation and adaptation co-benefits, the indicators are designed to be comprehensive and consistent with the logical framework allowing adequate coverage. The enabling environment and paradigm shift potential of the project is captured through a set of specific indicators that reflect the proposed Results Management Framework. Moreover, the project-specific indicators proposed by the AE are complementary and will measure context-specific results.

The annexed table in the body of the proposal package provides an overview of how the monitoring and evaluation will be operationalized, and the project will be reported on annually to the GCF Board. The annual reporting will include a summary of the total budget has been allotted to implement the various data collection and measurement tools while setting aside the budget for interim and final independent evaluations. In addition to the annual monitoring and evaluation reports, the AE will provide an annual progress report that will measure the various adaptation benefits that the direct and indirect beneficiaries receive.

## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018. The five-year accreditation term of the AE lapsed on 3 October 2019. As a result, the AE submitted an application for re-accreditation to the GCF on the date of the lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework was approved by the GCF Board. The GCF Board approved the revised accreditation of the AE pursuant to decision B.37/IR and negotiation of the amended and restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.

73\. The Fund has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

74\. The proposed project will be implemented in Iraq, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed.

75\. The funds of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the Independent Integrity Unit have both reported cases where GCF has been subject to litigation or expropriation, and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. It should therefore be noted that any decision by the Board to make only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country in question.

(a) Signature of the project agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP249

## Proposal name: Strengthening climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agriculture Livelihoods in Iraq (SRVALI)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

### Country: Republic of Iraq

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

The "Strengthening climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agriculture Livelihoods in Iraq (SRVALI)" project is a cross-cutting, small-scale, public-sector initiative designed for implementation by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) in the governorates of Karbala, Muthanna and Najaf. These regions are among the most vulnerable to climate change in Iraq due to their arid climate, high levels of food insecurity, and increasing water scarcity. The project aims to contribute to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions through the adoption of low-emission agricultural practices under Component 2 of the Climate Change Action Plan of the United Nations (FAO) through FAO's Small Grants Programme.

The SRVALI project is designed to address critical barriers to climate resilience in the agriculture sector, including lack of public investment, shortage of energy for efficient water use, high levels of food insecurity, and increasing water scarcity. The project's rationale is built on the need to improve water management, increase the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, and enhance the capacity of vulnerable populations for sustainable development. By focusing on the most vulnerable regions and populations, the project aims to create a paradigm shift in how water and agricultural resources are managed in Iraq, ultimately enhancing food security and reducing poverty.

The project is also expected to make a relatively small but significant contribution to climate change mitigation by achieving an estimated 100% reduction of CO2 emissions (tCO2 eq) over its 20-year lifetime, with 576.1 tCO2 eq reduced during the six-year implementation period. This includes only the anticipated GHG reductions from infrastructure investments and energy efficiency measures in the project area related to water supply systems as a result of the adoption of low-emission agricultural practices under Component 2 are considered. The project is also expected to make significant contributions to climate change adaptation by reducing vulnerability through improved water management, increased use of renewable energy sources, and enhanced capacity for sustainable development.

Furthermore, the project aims to directly benefit 1,046,800 individuals and indirectly benefit 913,234 individuals, reaching a total of 1,959,134 people (approximately 4.5 per cent of the population of Iraq), with 50 per cent being women. The specific adaptation result areas


---
**Page 115**
---

address include the resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities, health and well-being, and food and water security as well as infrastructure and built environment. By improving water management and promoting climate-resilient agriculture, the project aims to reduce the vulnerability of these communities.

5. The project has three main and complementary components:

(a) **Component 1:** Strengthening resilience against climate-induced water scarcity. This component focuses on increasing the efficiency of irrigation systems in the face of increasing water scarcity due to climate change. Key outputs include the conversion of open-air canals to piped systems (the biggest single project investment, at USD 20 million), the installation of solar-powered pumps and other renewable energy-powered systems, and capacity-building for water management. Activities will focus on reducing water losses in irrigation systems and increasing the availability of reliable water supply for agricultural production.

(b) **Component 2:** Climate-resilient agriculture production. This component focuses on addressing climate change impacts through climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies. Outputs include enhanced farmer capacities, adoption of climate-resilient crops, improved access to markets, increased use of weather-based insurance programmes, knowledge transfer initiatives, and the empowerment of Water User Associations (WUAs) at the local level.

(c) **Component 3:** Scaling-up climate adaptation through policy formulation and planning. This component aims to create an enabling environment for sustainable water and agricultural management in Iraq. Key outputs include strengthened policy frameworks and enhanced institutional capacities for climate change adaptation. Activities will involve the development of policies and strategies for water management, renewable energy adoption, and climate-resilient agriculture at the national level.

6. The SWALIM project is of critical importance to Iraq, not only from a socioeconomic standpoint, such as enhancing food security and livelihoods in rural areas, but also from an environmental perspective.

7. The total project budget is USD 38.95 million, with USD 29.25 million requested from GCF as a grant. Co-financing amounts to USD 9.70 million, comprising both in-cash contributions from the Iraqi government and in-kind contributions of USD 14.6 million for technical assistance and capacity-building support.

8. The project will be implemented by the Government of Iraq, through the Ministry of Water Resources and the Ministry of Environment, and FAO Iraq, which will oversee the implementation of GCF-funded activities. FAO's role as an executing entity (EE) ensures the project's alignment with national strategies and effective delivery to targeted stakeholders.

The project’s governance structure includes a project management unit that will oversee the implementation of activities, coordination among stakeholders and monitoring and evaluation. FAO, as the AE, will be responsible for ensuring compliance with GCF standards and policies in all aspects of the project. The Ministry of Water Resources (MWR) is responsible for the day-to-day operations of the project, ensuring alignment with national strategies and effective delivery of project outputs.

1.2 **Impact potential:** Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 116**
---

### 1.2.1 Adaptation impact potential

The project sets out to enhance climate resilience of vulnerable agriculture households in Kirkuk, Muthana and Najaf governorates, targeting 1,044,800 direct beneficiaries and 913,234 indirect beneficiaries, which represents about 4.5 per cent of the population of Iraq.

There is a need to consider the extent to which the project can address the extensive scale of climate vulnerability in Iraq; there may be challenges in extending these benefits more broadly across the country. The project should therefore focus on building resilience and capacity rather than a complete solution to the extensive adaptation needs of Iraq.

The project is well-positioned to reduce vulnerability by enhancing adaptive capacity, especially through the introduction of water-saving technologies such as drip irrigation. Water User Associations and introducing climate-resilient crops are appropriate given the context. However, the effectiveness of these interventions will depend on the ability of local communities to adapt flexibly to changes in weather patterns and a scale of local institutions, which can be challenging in regions with complex sociopolitical dynamics.

Strengthening institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning is a positive and necessary component of the project. The proposed policy and governance reforms align well with the Iraqi government’s National Climate Change Strategy. However, long-term sustainability of these reforms may hinge on the political and institutional stability of the country. The success of this reform will also depend on how it is implemented; this area will require ongoing support and possibly adaptive management as conditions evolve.

The project's efforts to increase the generation and use of climate information in decision-making are a positive step, particularly given Iraq’s limited capacity for climate change and enhancing data-driven agricultural planning. The project takes steps to empower communities through education and training on climate-resilient practices. However, if information across all stakeholder groups may face hurdles, especially in areas with limited infrastructure or access to education.

The project's efforts to enhance adaptive capacity and reduce exposure to climate risks is aligned with the country’s urgent needs. By improving water management and agricultural practices, reducing soil erosion, increasing crop yields, minimizing water leakage and increasing efficiency of water use, the project aims to reduce the risk of crop failure and enhance food security. While these efforts are essential, the complexity of climate challenges in Iraq suggests that a more integrated and holistic approach is required potentially enhancing overall resilience.

Education and capacity-building as part of this effort and risk reduction measures within communities is a critical aspect of the project. The focus on education and capacity-building is likely to yield positive outcomes, particularly in raising awareness among vulnerable populations regarding the severity of climate change and addressing cultural and social barriers, which requires a nuanced and context-sensitive approach.

### 1.2.2 Mitigation impact potential

The project is expected to reduce 22,536 t CO₂ eq over its 20-year lifetime, contributing to the mitigation efforts of Iraq. This reduction is achieved through a combination of two infrastructure projects: (i) installation of solar water pumps and replacement of diesel-powered water pumps resulting in a decreased need for pumping water; and installation of solar systems on water control structures. The project also includes the decommissioning of power generation resources of the Ministry of Water Resources and substituting for fossil-fuel-based energy from the grid. The project is expected to reduce 20,536 t CO₂ eq over its lifetime. While this level of carbon reduction is a positive contribution, it represents a modest percentage of overall emissions in Iraq, highlighting the importance of viewing this project as part of a broader, long-term strategy


---
**Page 117**
---

The project also aspires to achieve mitigation co-benefits, at a much larger scale, through Component 2 interventions to shift to low-emission agricultural practices in barley, wheat and cotton production systems. The Secretariat had recommended that these be considered mitigation co-benefits rather than key results.

The project’s focus on avoiding the lock-in of high-emission infrastructure by promoting solar powered irrigation systems is forward-looking. This shift towards renewable energy in agriculture can help Iraq to reduce its dependence on imported fossil fuels, and stand to reduce the need for pumping water that is subsequently lost. However, the success of this initiative will depend upon the ability of the project to support farmers in maintaining and scaling these systems in the context of a broader energy landscape for Iraq.

The installation of solar panels for irrigation contributes to low-emission energy capacity, which can be used to replace fossil fuel-based pumping systems. The contribution for renewable energy use in agriculture, its overall contribution to energy transition in Iraq may be limited by the scale and scope of this project. However, it is hoped that integrating these efforts with national energy strategies could enhance the long-term impact.

Using the EX-ACT tool, the project conducted an estimation of GHG emission reductions according to the mitigation co-benefits from Component 1, and for the mitigation co-benefits from Component 2. In relation to Component 2; the ITAP identified certain limitations in the assessment of irrigation systems and the consideration of a constant value for soil organic carbon. These limitations, which could impact the accuracy of the results, were acknowledged by the AE, who has committed to addressing them in the monitoring and evaluation framework, and making the necessary adjustments both in annual reporting and in future versions of the tool.

The SWALI project is a valuable and necessary initiative for Iraq, addressing critical adaptation needs. By focusing on water management, increasing irrigation infrastructure capacity and the broader context; the project is well-positioned to make meaningful contributions to climate resilience and low-emission development pathways in Iraq.

Overall, the ITAP rates the climate impact potential of the proposed project as medium to high.


## Paradigm shift potential

The project introduces a potentially game-changing shift away from the traditional system of irrigation through open-air canals in Iraq; to closed piped systems, significantly reducing water loss by evaporation and seepage. This is achieved through innovative solutions for modernizing the system of water regulation and use, including the introduction of metering systems, backed up by strengthened community-based water resource management institutions. The project also introduces climate-resilient agricultural practices, which are expected to create new market opportunities in Iraq’s agricultural sector. However, the successful implementation and deployment of these technologies; the project opens avenues for new business models and market creation. The success of this initiative will depend upon the ability of farmers to adopt these innovations. Moreover, the scalability of these innovations may require further support to ensure broader market penetration beyond the initial project sites.


---
**Page 118**
---

24. The project documents indicate the development of a robust M&E system, which are essential for fostering broader adoption of successful practices. These mechanisms are designed to capture insights and experiences from the pilot phase and ensure that lessons learned can be scaled up. This will be implemented with the support of certain university partners in the country, among others, to contribute to the overall institutional capacity building on climate-resilient agriculture and knowledge management and the potential for incorporating lessons into other initiatives is a strength. The project also highlights the importance of ensuring the availability of information dissemination and the willingness of other stakeholders to adopt these lessons.

25. The project has arrangements in place that could support the long-term and financially sustainable scaling up of the project’s activities, including the involvement of local institutions, capacity-building efforts, the creation of strategic partnerships and, most importantly, testing new ways of financially contributing to the sustainability of irrigation systems. These include the development of a “pay-as-you-go” (PAYG) irrigation fees (based on hours of irrigation and not on how much water is received) towards volumetric measurements and metering infrastructure. The project also highlights that it will be important to enhance equity within irrigation systems as some farmers may currently pay without receiving water for their crops.

26. However, the sustainability of these outcomes will likely depend on the continued commitment of local stakeholders and the availability of financial resources, which could be challenging in the long term. In addition, the project documents indicate that there are barriers to the implementation phase of the project; the ITAP believes that the exit strategy could be strengthened by including a more detailed discussion of how these challenges will be addressed. On this subject, the entity, during the question-and-answer session, promised to explore this possibility.

27. The project aims to shift incentives in favor of low-carbon and climate-resilient development by addressing key barriers such as the high costs and risks of adopting new technologies and practices. The project documents indicate that the entity is currently working to encourage broader adoption among market participants. The potential impact is significant, though it remains unclear how this will translate into widespread change, notably for the domestic private sector. On this aspect, the project lacks clarity.

28. The project is well-positioned to advance national and local regulatory frameworks that promote investment in low-emission and climate-resilient development. The focus on policy reforms is a strength of the project as it could have significant impacts beyond its immediate scope. However, the success of these efforts will depend on the stability of political and institutional frameworks, as well as the capacity of local governments to implement and enforce these regulations effectively.

29. The project’s theory of change for replication is well-articulated, suggesting that the proposed approach could be scaled up in other contexts with similar characteristics. However, harnessing this potential for replication could catalyse broader impacts beyond its immediate scope of the project, especially if successes are communicated effectively and adapted to other contexts. The challenge will be to ensure that these efforts can be replicated successfully at scale both technically and financially.

30. The project’s theory demonstrates good potential to catalyse broader impacts beyond its immediate scope, particularly through innovative volumetric measurements and tariff system, market creation and policy influence. The realization of this potential will depend on effectively addressing the challenges related to implementation in a context that is different from the pilot environment in Iraq.


---
**Page 119**
---

1.4 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project offers a range of environmental, social and economic co-benefits that align with several United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These include SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 2 (Zero Hunger), SDG 3 (Gender Equality), SDG 13 (Climate Action), and SDG 15 (Life on Land). The project contributes to sustainable development, contributing to both immediate and long-term goals in the targeted regions.

In addition to the mitigation on benefits discussed above, the project promotes positive environmental impacts. These include improved water quality through enhanced water conservation of water resources and protection of biodiversity. By implementing low-emission climate change adaptation measures such as rainwater harvesting systems and irrigation, it helps reduce soil degradation, prevents over-extraction of water resources, and promotes the sustainable use of land. The introduction of water-saving technologies is crucial for maintaining the environmental health of the targeted regions, through their contribution to the consistent and widespread adoption of these practices.

The project also contributes to improved food security and safety outcomes for both women and men by reducing vulnerability to climate-induced risks and promoting safer agricultural practices. The introduction of drought-resistant crops and improved irrigation systems reduces the risk associated with water scarcity and agricultural failures, thereby contributing to better health and food security outcomes. To the extent that metering and volumetric tariffs are implemented in the targeted regions, they will also contribute to improved water management.

The project is expected to generate positive externalities in terms of job creation and poverty alleviation, particularly for rural communities, including both women and men. By enhancing agricultural productivity through the introduction of climate-resilient crops and irrigation systems, it creates new employment opportunities in the agriculture sector and related industries. Additionally, by promoting the development of small-scale agro-processing industries, these could contribute to long-term economic growth and resilience in the targeted regions.

The project places a strong emphasis on addressing gender inequalities and empowering women through capacity-building activities that promote their leadership roles. Through targeted capacity-building activities and improved access to financing, the project seeks to ensure that women are better equipped to participate in and benefit from the project's benefits disproportionately affected by climate change, are actively involved in and benefit from the project's outcomes.

The SRVALI project demonstrates strong potential to contribute to sustainable development in Iraq, with a focus on environmental conservation, economic growth and social inclusion. The IEAP rates the sustainable development potential of the proposed project as high.


1.5 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

Iraq faces significant exposure to climate risks, including increased temperatures, erratic rainfall and water scarcity. The country's agricultural sector, particularly in the southern governorates of Kirkuk, Muthanna and Najaf, is highly vulnerable to these climate risks. The intensity and frequency of extreme weather events are expected to increase as a result of targeted interventions like the SRVALI project to build resilience in these regions.


---
**Page 120**
---

### Country ownership

#### Scale: Medium to high

The SIVVAL project is closely aligned with national priorities for low-emission and climate-resilient development, as outlined in key national documents such as the updated nationally determined contributions (NDCs) submitted under the Paris Agreement. The project enhances agricultural resilience and promoting renewable energy solutions directly supports national strategies while demonstrating strong alignment with the strategic objectives and technology needs of Iraq.

The development of the SIVVAL project involved extensive consultations with a wide range of stakeholders, including government agencies, civil society organizations, and local communities. Particular attention was given to ensuring that gender equality and social inclusion were integrated into all aspects of the project design and implementation. Strengthening stakeholder engagement is critical for maintaining transparency, accountability, and local ownership throughout the project's implementation. To strengthen the stakeholder engagement process in the future, it would be important to revisit the criteria for selecting traditional practices and technologies that are sustainable and culturally appropriate while avoiding the adoption of ignoring practices and techniques that hold strong cultural value for the farmers.

The project’s alignment with broader development and climate strategies in Iraq has been reinforced by its close collaboration with key national institutions such as the Ministry of Water Resources and the Ministry of Environment. These institutions play a central role in the implementation of SIVVAL, ensuring that the project is aligned with national priorities while addressing climate change and sustainable development. Their involvement also enhances the project's legitimacy and credibility within Iraq.

FAO, as both the AE and EE, brings a wealth of relevant experience and expertise to the project. FAO, as per the information provided in the Funding Proposal, has a long history of working in Iraq and has successfully implemented similar projects focused on agriculture, water


---
**Page 121**
---

### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

The financial structure of the project demonstrates an appropriate level of concessionality designed to make the project viable without distorting market conditions.

- The use of grant funding is justified given economic constraints in Iraq and the public good nature of the project, which aims to provide a long-term solution for vulnerable small-scale farming communities.
  
The project is structured in a way that minimizes the risk of crowding out private and other public investments by focusing on areas where private sector involvement is limited due to high risk or low immediate returns. GCF support plays a catalytic role rather than displacing other resources, ensuring that the project complements existing and potential future investments in agricultural and water sectors in Iraq.

The co-finance of USD 9.7 million demonstrates some commitment from the Government of Iraq and other partners, like FAO. The co-financing is crucial for ensuring sustainability of the project. The inclusion of a co-financer with concessional terms also indicates effective leveraging of GCF resources to maximize impact, though the overall level of co-funding remains low compared to the sector’s potential.

The project represents best practices and lessons learned from similar initiatives, both globally and within the region, to ensure maximum efficiency and effectiveness. The conversion of open land into productive agricultural land in Iraq is a specific example of climate-resilient crops, and community-based water management practices reflect the application of well-established best practices for sustainable agriculture.

The project’s emphasis on innovation, particularly in the reduction of evaporation losses through the use of drip irrigation systems, the introduction of renewable energy to manage water, as well as the use of renewable energy, climate-resilient agricultural practices, and the establishment of a group of farmers’ association as an example of change, aligns with best practice and contributes to the project’s goals of efficiency and effectiveness. The introduction of these innovations in a challenging context like Iraq requires careful planning and execution, but the potential for long-term benefits is significant.

The overall economic rate of return for the project (estimated at 16 per cent) when considering GCF support, indicates a positive outcome that justifies the investment. The project’s design ensures that the resources are used effectively to generate both immediate and longer-term benefits, thereby demonstrating the potential for sustainable and improved interventions.


---
**Page 122**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The iTAP project is designed with a sound financial structure and incorporates best practices to ensure efficient and effective use of resources. The co-financing commitment and potential for catalyzing further investments are strengths, although the economic constraints in Iraq present challenges. Despite these, the project is well-positioned to deliver significant and sustainable impacts with GCF support.

Consequently, the iTAP considers the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project.

#### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The proposed project has a comprehensive and strategic approach to enhancing climate resilience and promoting food security in vulnerable regions of Iraq. Furthermore, the project demonstrates strong alignment with national priorities and strategies. The inclusion of farmer field schools as an integral component is well-positioned to significantly improve water management, agricultural resilience, and policy frameworks, thereby benefiting nearly 2 million individuals, including a substantial proportion of women.

However, the iTAP suggests that the AE considers the following aspects:

- Seek strong involvement from academic stakeholders in the country to integrate into both the project design and implementation phases. This will ensure the inclusion of tools that can help address the gaps in the EX-ACT tool.
- Develop, after the mid-term evaluation, a sustainability plan for the project that would include alternative funding sources for activities initiated by the project and avenues for greater involvement of the domestic private sector.
- During the design phase, develop an outreach and farmer engagement plan to take into account the cultural preferences of farmers among other factors in the selection of techniques and practices to be considered in the farmer field schools.

Based on the analysis presented in the above paragraphs, the iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Gender Assessment

## Introduction

The objective of the gender assessment is to facilitate and inform gender mainstreaming in the Full Funding Proposal to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for the project "Sustainable Rural Livelihoods in Iraq's rural communities [SRVALI]." The assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation and dynamics in Iraq, with a particular focus on the role of women in agriculture. It also examines how climate change is affecting women’s livelihoods and their capacity in adapting to it. It seeks to identify key gender issues in climate change adaptation and strategies for addressing them. The assessment was conducted through interviews with experts from relevant national policies, plans, research studies, donor initiatives, and stakeholder consultations.

The scope of the assessment has been limited by the time available and the availability of data on the impact of climate change on women in Iraq. The findings of the assessment have been used to make recommendations for addressing gender issues within the project. Part I of this report provides an overview of the gender situation in Iraq, while Part II provides the Gender Action Plan for the project.

## Key Expert Interviews & Consultations

A needs assessment was conducted with experts (KEIs) to understand the gender dimensions of the impact of climate change on agriculture, initiatives to address these impacts, and the role of women. The interviews were carried out in August 2022 and March 2023 with co-financers of the project, the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency (SIDA), and the Global Alliance for Climate-Smart Agriculture (GAC). The consultations also included representatives from the following organizations: World Food Programme (WFP), United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), UN Women, International Organization for Migration (IOM), Iraqi Council for Women’s Affairs (ICWA), Iraqi Council for Children’s Rights (ICCRI), Iraqi Council for Social Development (ICSD), Iraqi Council for Women’s Cooperation Agency (ICJCA), non-governmental organizations (NGOs) working with women in Iraq, and the Ministry of Planning, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Environment and Directorate of Water Resources. The findings from these interviews have informed the Gender Assessment and the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

Further consultations were also carried out from August 2022 to March 2023 with the co-financers of the project, the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency (SIDA), and the Global Alliance for Climate-Smart Agriculture (GAC). The consultations were in line with the Gender Equality Policy of both donors and the consultations led to the financing and the start-up of the initiatives "Enhance climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural households" (SIDA initiative – USD 10.2 million total budget) and "Promoting women’s economic empowerment through climate change by empowering women as agents for transformation and addressing the food-energy-water nexus" (GAC initiative - USD 7.3 million total budget). The two projects are co-financing the SRVALI with USD 4.9 million and USD 1.7 million respectively (see Annex 13).

String synergies and complementarities exist in particular with both GAC’s "Gender Equality Policy" and the “Canada Feminist International Policy” and with SIDA's policy "Promoting Gender Equality."


---
**Page 129**
---

Community Consultations

4. Community consultations<sup>1</sup> were held in the governorates of Kerbala, Najaf, and Muthanna, to ensure that the project design was responsive and relevant to the needs of women and men farmers in the target areas. The purpose of the consultations was to gain information on new technologies and innovations needed by farmers in adapting to climate change. The consultations explored women's and men's roles in agricultural production, access to land, water, seeds, inputs, markets for their livelihoods, access to resources and extension services, and participation in decision-making. Feedback on the concept of Climate Wise Women (CWW) and key areas where women need support were also collected.

The methods of engagement included focus group discussions (FGDs), in-depth interviews (IDIs), and key informant interviews (KIIIs), differentiated by gender. In total, nine FGDs with women farmers (one in each governorate), nine IDIs with women-headed households, and three KIIIs were conducted. The consultations also involved meetings with men (see Table 1: Community Consultation Distribution of FGDs, KII and IDIs). A total of 72 women and 25 men were reached through these consultations. The final report, which contains the main findings from this research, is attached as Annex 6A.

Demographic Profile

5. Iraq's population is estimated to be around 39,172,869 million, of which 50.5 percent is male and 49.5 percent is female.<sup>2</sup> Women comprise nearly half of the total population and head one in ten Iraqi households; 80 percent of these are widows.<sup>3</sup> Some 70

*Iraq's population is composed of several ethnic and religious minorities, including Christians, Kurds, Turkmenis, Assyrians and Yazidis. According to Minority Groups International, Iraq has a total population of 35 million people, with approximately three are Shi'a Arabs, Sunni Arabs and Kurds, who are mainly following Sunni Islam. It is estimated that 99 percent of Iraqis are Muslim, divided among Shi'a and Sunni; and the remainder are Christian, Yazidi, Sufi, and other religious minorities such as Assyrian Christians, Yezidis, Kaka'i and Saeen-Mandanis. Around 12.3 percent households are polygamous, with an estimated 40% of these being wives of their husbands.* An estimated 0.6 million are people with disabilities.*

In 2020, Iraq was labeled as "very high risk" of a humanitarian crisis by INFORM's GHI Index. The most vulnerable groups in terms of access to and quality of delivery of humanitarian assistance are those directly affected by the 2014-2017 conflict against Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant (ISIL), which displaced nearly six million people. Humanitarian needs include food, shelter, water, sanitation, health care, education, protection, sanitation, education, and livelihoods opportunities. Of the people in acute need (1.77 million), 50 percent are concentrated in only two governorates — Ninewa and Al-Anbar, and of these, 42% live in camps or IDP settlements. Among those who have remained internally displaced, 70 percent of whom have been displaced for more than three years; 61 percent of the population living in camps are children under age five. Approximately 537,000 IDPs in out-of-camp locations and 1,750,000 returnees face critical problems related to resilience and recovery. About 27 percent of IDPs are unemployed, and within this group, 48% have been displaced for more than five years; 63% of whom are less than 18 years old.*


**Poverty**

Overall: 20 percent of Iraqis were living under the poverty line before the onset of COVID-19 in 2020. While, the historically poorer South still had the highest poverty

9. As a result of COVID-19 pandemic and associated socio-economic impacts, 4.5 million (11.7 percent) Iraqis have been pushed below the poverty line. Significant job losses and rising unemployment rates are the main drivers of this increase in poverty compared to pre-crisis levels. In 2018, with an additional 15.8 percent falling into poverty, children are the most impacted group. The ratio of children living under the poverty line has increased from 37 percent in 2018. With an additional 15.8 percent falling into poverty, children are the most impacted group. The ratio of children living under the poverty line has increased from 37 percent in 2018. With an additional 15.8 percent falling into poverty, children are the most impacted group. The ratio of children living under the poverty line has increased to almost double to 6 out of 10 children (37.9 percent) as the crisis unfolds. About 42 percent of the population is vulnerable, facing a higher risk as they are dependent on informal employment and remittances for income generation and financial security. Disruption to public services and the adoption of negative coping strategies by households will likely increase deprivation in wellbeing and inequality, especially among children.

### Human Development Index and Gender Inequality Index

10. Iraq ranked 123rd out of 189 countries on the UN's Human Development Index in 2020. Iraq is also ranked 146th (out of 189) on the Global Gender Gap Report, which measures gender inequalities in three important aspects of human development: health (measured by life expectancy at birth and maternal mortality rates); empowerment (parliamentary seats occupied by women and proportion of adult women and men aged 25 years and older with at least some secondary education); and economic participation and opportunity (female labor force participation rate and female and male populations). Iraq holds the lowest GII ranking among all countries in the Middle East and North Africa region, as Saudi Arabia (60), Oman (68), Lebanon (96), Algeria (103) and Iran (113). However, it is still better positioned than Syria (122), Afghanistan (157) and Yemen (162).

### Education

11. In Iraq, the national literacy rate is nearly 82 percent and the illiteracy rate (six years and older) is near 18 percent. Nearly 70 percent women (15–49 years old) are literate, which is 74.3 percent in urban areas and 56.4 percent in rural areas, and 78 percent men are literate, which is 82.5 percent in urban areas and 73.5 percent in rural areas.

12. The MICS 2018 findings show disparities in enrollment and completion rates by gender, area, and wealth. According to the latest Iraq Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey (MICS) 2018, the enrollment rate of boys exceeds the enrollment rate of girls in the primary stage and in later stages, except in secondary education, where the enrollment rate for girls (35 percent) is higher than the enrollment rate for boys (31 percent).


---
**Page 132**
---

13. There are two million out of school children in the country, half of which should be attending primary education. Moreover, results indicate that 68 percent girls and boys whose parents have completed secondary or higher education attend only childhood or primary education programmes. This percentage drastically decreases with children of lower secondary school age who are not attending primary or secondary programmes, boys (35 percent) and girls (10 percent).

### Health

14. The maternal mortality ratio in Iraq is 79 per 100,000 live births.28 The MICS findings indicate that the coverage of pregnant women is relatively high in Iraq, where 87.5 percent aged 15-49 years have at least one ante-natal (ANC) visit and 67.9 percent received four or more ANC visits during their pregnancy. However, only 30.2 percent delivered at a health facility. There has been a marked improvement in trends in under-five mortality rates. While in 1995, the rate was 45.8 deaths per 1,000 live births, in 2018, the ratio was 36.7 deaths per 1,000 live births. The MICS findings indicate that women with secondary education or above are reducing the mortality rate of children under the age of five with an average of 24 deaths per thousand live births compared to those who have no secondary education and above, compared to 27 deaths thousand live births of mothers with primary education or none.

### Women's Economic Participation

15. Women make essential contributions to the local economy in general and rural economy in particular, yet their access to productive resources and employment opportunities is limited. This limits women’s ability to improve their own lives and to better contribute to the economic growth, food security and sustainable development of their communities and countries.74 Women in Iraq are not accessing the labour market at a rate that reflects their share of the population. According to the 2018 Labour Force Survey (LFS), women’s participation in the workforce was compared to 74.1 percent of men.75 Moreover, women are excluded from the industry sector represented only by 3.9 percent (mostly related to the oil sector) compared to men (26.7 percent). Women represent a very small percentage of agricultural workers (mostly informally and with poor social protection), accounting 43.9 percent of labour compared to 12.1 percent of men.76 In 2011, the percentage of women in agricultural employment was 45 percent; while men represented 17.1 percent. In 2012, 43.7


---
**Page 133**
---

16. The unemployment rate for young women is double that of men. In 2017, about 56 percent of young women were unemployed compared to 29 percent for young men. This gender gap in employment is also reflected in the private sector, where only two percent of the total number of employees are women, most of whom are employed in low-paid and low-skilled jobs. There is a lack of information on the employment status of women who work informally or in agricultural and domestic work.

|---|---|

| Employment in Industry | 3.5 | 3.9 |
| Employment in Services | 67.5 | 62.3 |

Source: World Bank, Iraq Demographic and Health Survey, 2017.

17. Women in Iraq carry much of the burden of unpaid household work. Most care work, such as cleaning, cooking, and caring for children or elderly, is performed by women and girls. According to a study conducted by the Overseas Development Institute (ODI) in 2012 reported that women spend on average more than six hours a day performing such activities, such as cooking and child care. According to the Overseas Development Institute (ODI), women's unpaid work is estimated at $3 billion per year, which is equivalent to 5 percent of Iraq's GDP. The World Bank estimates an increase from one to three hours "to most of the day," with women continuing to consistently invest most of the time and energy (men spent only 25 percent). In addition, women in conflict-affected areas regard care work as their fundamental responsibility and part of their identity, while men are more likely to be involved in income-generating activities. Women and elderly women are the ones that mostly engage in unpaid care work.

18. Among reasons cited by women for not seeking employment are domestic responsibilities, lack of education and training, cultural expectations, a perceived lack of qualifications, lack of access to safe transportation, opposition from family members, and health issues. In addition, if through


---
**Page 134**
---

employment, a woman can keep and use her income as she sees fit, this may also be perceived as undermining the man’s traditional authority in the home.

### Socio-Economic Profile of Rural Women in Iraq

19. Rural women constitute about a third of Iraq's women population. More than half of the women aged 12 years and above are married (56.5 percent), 35.2 percent are single, 0.2 percent are widowed, and 8.1 percent are divorced or separated. The percentage of women-headed households at the national level is 9.9 percent, 10.3 percent in urban areas, and 7.4 percent in rural areas. In 2016, 5.2 percent (1.2 percent women [15–49 years old]) in rural areas own a mobile phone (72.4 percent in the urban area). The rise in female-headed households, from 7.7 percent in 2010 to nearly one in ten in 2016, is attributed to the increasing number of women-headed households due to deteriorating security situation and the escalation of violence in the country. Immigration also plays a role in the pattern of social and economic relations with the loss of the family’s breadwinner.

### Women in Rural Areas

20. In rural areas are likely to be more economically active than women in urban areas, but they face many challenges. They have limited access to education and update more in the informal—and therefore unprotected by Iraq's equal opportunities law—economy sphere. Uneducated women in rural areas have a much higher incidence of poverty compared with their educated counterparts (see Table 3: Social Status of Iraqi Women Disaggregated by Urban and Rural). Logistical and security issues contribute to rural women’s inability to access education and, in turn, limit their economic opportunities.

### Discrimination and Barriers

21. Discriminatory social norms often prevent women from inheriting or acquiring land and limit their access to services (be it health, social, financial or agricultural support services). Women are also subject to gender-based violence, which is a major mobility constraint also undermine their participation in decision-making processes and their involvement in the provision of public goods, services, and participating in decision-making processes at the community level.

21. Rural women shoulder a disproportionate number of roles and responsibilities, which include childcare, domestic chores, agricultural work (such as farming, gardening, and livestock rearing) and suffer from gender-based discrimination in accessing social services, making them more vulnerable to the effects of poverty. They have limited access to education, health care, financial services, and other basic services and are gravely underpaid. Illiteracy and child marriage are prevalent among rural women. In nearly 40 percent of Iraqi households, women are responsible for providing access to education, fertility, drinking water, and sanitation (see Table 3: Social Status of Iraq Women Disaggregated by Urban and Rural). National efforts to target and include rural economic development programs have been hampered by a lack of funding for small-scale businesses or projects, which could generate both income or employment in the area.

## Political Participation

**Page 137**

26. Women are underrepresented in political decision-making at national and local levels, and within independent and reconciliation committees. The 2005 Constitution provides for a quota of 25 percent representation of women in the national Parliament. Despite the adoption of the Political Parties Law No 36 of 2015, which stipulates women's participation in political parties at least equal to men’s participation, women's representation in political parties is still inadequate, as the law does not include provisions for the establishment of quotas or gender balance within political parties. However, The May 2019 parliamentary elections saw an unprecedented number of women running for elections, nearly 2,600. As of February 2021, 264 percent of women were elected to Parliament, with 58 women out of a total of 329 seats allocated to women. This indicates society's increasing confidence to elect women and their ability to overcome electoral challenges including defamation, intimidation, and harassment, which led to the withdrawal of some of the candidates from the electoral race.

Legal Status of Women

27 Iraq’s Constitution (adopted in 2005) states that all Iraqis are equal before the law and prohibits discrimination based on sex (Article 14). The Iraqi Constitution guarantees basic human rights for all citizens, including women. It provides equality between male and female Iraqi citizens and further states that they shall have the right to participate in public affairs and to enjoy political rights, including the right to vote, elect, and run for election. Article 20 of the constitution guarantees the right of the family—especially children and women—to social and health security, the basic requirements of life, education, and decent life, and shall secure for them suitable income and appropriate housing (1).

In addition, the following Articles of the Constitution are relevant to protection from gender-based discrimination: Article 23 prohibits all forms of discrimination in matters based on gender, race, ethnicity, origin, colour, religion, creed, belief or opinion, or economic and social status (Article 14). Every individual has the right to enjoy life, security, and freedom. The state shall ensure the protection of human rights in accordance with the law and based on a decision issued by a competent judicial authority. Article 25 guarantees the right of every person to preserve his entity and its ethical, moral, and patriotic values. The State guarantees the protection of motherhood, childhood and old age and shall care for children and youth and provide them with education and training in accordance with their capabilities. All forms of violence and abuse in the Republic of Iraq, the family, school, and society shall be prohibited (Article 26). Article 37 prohibits all forms of trafficking in women or children, and sex trafficking are prohibited (Article 37). The Constitution, however, does not mention the most important rights for women: family-related rights, such as marriage, the right to choose a spouse, divorce, custody, guardianship, and inheritance.

28. However, the Constitution also cites Islam as the basic source of legislation and forbids the enactment of laws contradictory to its “established rulings,” and Article 41 allows each religious group in Iraq to govern its own personal status matters. As


---
**Page 138**
---

result, the situation of women in Iraq very much depends on the implementation of Islamic law and on the priorities and interpretations of male-led religious authorities.

### National Policies and Strategies for Gender Equality

29. There are several government policies and strategies aimed at promoting and protecting women's employment and economic empowerment such as the National Strategy for Women's Development (NSWD) 2015-2030, the Council Resolution (UNSCR) 1325 (NAPR 1325), the 2014-2018 National Strategy for the Advancement of Women in Iraq, and the Law No. 29/2016 on Paid Maternity Leave.

These laws provide paid maternity leave, prohibit discrimination against women during recruitment and in the workplace, and increase female participation in the public sphere. However, the implementation and enforcement of these policies is inconsistent, particularly in the private sector where there are no specific provisions for the employment of rural women and neither is there a gender strategy in place for the agricultural sector.

30. The National Development Plan highlights the low participation of women in economic activities and calls on the government to empower women's civil societies and political institutions as one of the key challenges to development. The empowerment of women in terms of health, economics, science and security and increasing their participation in decision-making is a priority for Iraq.

The National Strategy on Violence against Women and Girls 2016-2030 provides an overall framework within which policy and decision-makers work to take concrete actions aimed at preventing violence against women and girls and ending the impunity of violence.

31. Iraq ratified the most important international treaty related to gender equality, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1986, but has yet to ratify its Optional Protocol on violence against women. The law No. 29/2016 on Paid Maternity Leave is a step towards human rights that have become universal, and is often described as an international bill of rights for women.

The National Strategy on Violence against Women and Girls 2016-2030 codifies discrimination against women and sets up an agenda for national action to end such discrimination. Although Iraq is a party to numerous international human rights conventions, it has not yet ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). Iraq's current discriminatory legislative provisions illustrate the need for further reforms in order to bring its laws into conformity with their rights. Legislative change, coupled with active enforcement mechanisms, remains necessary to bring Iraq into full compliance with antidiscrimination instruments and ensure women's equal rights.


---
**Page 139**
---

32. The Constitution of the Republic of Iraq declares that Islam is the official religion of the State, a fundamental source of legislation, and that no law can contravene the established provisions of Islam. This provision is used to justify reservations to CEDAW.4 Despite ratifying CEDAW in 1986, Iraq maintains reservations to Article 2(2) and (3), which prohibit States parties from imposing any restrictions on women that discriminate against women; Article 9, which requires equal rights regarding changes and termination of marriage or family relations; and Article 29, paragraph 1, with regard to the principles of international arbitration on the interpretation or application of the convention. Iraq has also not ratified the Optional Protocol to CEDAW, which commits States parties to ensure that women enjoy their human rights without discrimination in all areas of public life, including marriage and family relations; Article 29, paragraph 1, with regard to the principles of international arbitration on the interpretation or application of the convention. 34. According to the Humanitarian Needs Overview Iraq (2020) (HNO), 1,29 million people are at risk of gender-based violence (GBV) in Iraq. Of these in need, 84 percent are women, 39 percent are children, five percent are older persons and five percent are people with disabilities. Furthermore, it is also noted that 98 percent of the GBV survivors do not report their cases to authorities due to fear of retaliation or because of domestic violence followed by forced/child marriages. However, reporting is quite limited in Iraq as a result of the lack of awareness about GBV and the existence of stigma and mistrust in available services and avenues for legal redress, as well as the potential for further violence.

35. Domestic violence against women and girls remain endemic in 2020, including the killings of women and girls by their families and husbands. While Iraq’s Criminal Code criminalizes physical assault, article 41(1) gives a husband a legal right to “punish” his wife with any means he deems necessary for her correction and reform, without question.” The Penal Code also provides for mitigated sentences for violent acts, including murder, if committed by a husband against his wife. In addition, the law criminalizes the act of adultery or sex outside of marriage. Iraqi parliamentary efforts to pass a draft law anti-domestic violence law have been ongoing since 2018 and were reinvigorated in response for domestic violence survivors, protection (restraining) orders, penalties for their breach, and other measures to address the issue of domestic violence. However, the bill has several gaps and provisions that would undermine its effectiveness, including that it prioritizes reconciliation over protection and justice for victims.

Impact of COVID-19

36. Since the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic in late February 2020 in Iraq, it has brought about a surge in unemployment and critical food security concerns for women and men. The pandemic has resulted in a significant drop in both in-kind and cash transfers. Moreover, Iraq faced significant challenges accessing both market and healthcare services during the pandemic. In March 2020, only 15 percent of households had access to markets, while only a small share of children received any catch-up or learning activities during school closures. The unemployment rate increased significantly in April (above 34 percent) compared to the pre-lockdown period (above 61 percent throughput). The unemployment rate increased significantly during the pandemic. Compared to 12.7 percent in March, it reached a peak of 50.8 percent in May and then decreased to 39.7 percent in August and then fell to 23.7 percent in September, and 22 percent in October. After the initial increase in unemployment during April, the rate remained high and stable among women. Pre-pandemic public sector workers were most successful in holding onto their jobs. Compared to more than 30 percent of private sector workers who lost their jobs or went out of the labor force between March and June, only about one-third (12 percent) of workers in the public sector had lost their jobs permanently or temporarily or gotten out of the labor force by August. However, the rate of unemployment among women in the public sector was out of the labor force in August. An estimated 6.0, 5.2, and 5.1 percent of Iraqis consumed inadequate diets in August, September, and October respectively. The


---
**Page 141**
---

37. According to the GBV Sub-Cluster Rapid Assessment on the Impact of COVID-19 Outbreak on GBV in Iraq, COVID-19 has increased the risk of GBV in Iraq through various factors such as: (i) lack of access to health services and other government measures; the GBV survivors might face challenges in accessing the lifesaving GBV services including safe shelters. This is also even more striking, as there is no possibility for women and girls to seek help from their families or communities that protects the survivors. Furthermore, it has been noted that resources have been directed to health systems and other essential services, which may lead to a decrease in income, loss of livelihoods due to economic consequences of COVID-19 pandemic can have direct impact on women as it might increase the risk of exploitation and sexual violence. Loss of financial resources can also lead to increased domestic violence, including intra-household conflict. Lastly, the crisis can increase the burden of women and girls who are caring for children and other family members. This may also contribute to an increased risk of infection. This is also valid for girls, whose schools are closed and who might be undertaking additional caregiving roles.

38. The review of 100 GBV cases in Iraq from April-June 2020 in 110 assessed sub-districts by conducting 1,442 key informant interviews on June 2020 has also showed that main protection risks affecting women and girls are psychological trauma (86 percent), stress and anxiety (79 percent) and lack of access to health services (53 percent). Lack of space and privacy (36 percent) and violence or abuse within families/households (23 percent) were the other main protection risks. The review also showed an increase in the severity of these issues. Furthermore, there has been increased reports of GBV such as domestic violence, self-immolation, self-inflicted injuries due to spousal abuse, sexual harassment of minors and suicide, and transactional sex.

Gender Norms

39. There is a persistence of deep-rooted patriarchal attitudes and stereotypes regarding women roles and responsibilities, which discriminate against women, and is exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. The root causes of gender discrimination are due to the persistent cultural and social tribal norms often witnessed by misconceptions about women’s reproductive investments being limited to men, as well as their economic power, role and barriers that prevent them from fully exercising women's rights and responsibilities. Furthermore, face women several legal and cultural restrictions in accessing education, health services, employment opportunities to move around freely, access education, jobs, land, and micro-credit finance. Such restrictions may be linked with women’s role in traditional societies, which often revolve around a father, brother or husband as the main decision-maker. The evidence shows that women are more likely to be involved in food production and preparation.

leaders of the household leads men to generally make the decisions for the household,
and on behalf of the women. The male head of household may make unilateral
decisions that impact a woman's entire life, including her participation in the job

include: preventing her from leaving home (60.5 percent) and going out alone

Women's economic participation can be a highly contested issue because it
contradicts the traditional men-as-breadwinners concept. Unpaid care work remains a
barrier for women's full enjoyment of their human rights and to participate fully in
society, which force women to stay in the home, limits their participation in the public sphere and prevents them from exercising their right to education and employment. The lack of
unpaid care work on women, especially women in poverty, is a barrier to women's full
enjoyment of their human rights, and this institutionalized inequality needs to be
addressed.

40. Community decisions are made by various groups, including mukhtars,
community leaders, and religious and/or tribal leaders. Most of these community level decision-
makers are men, which limits the representation of women in decision-making processes.
While some representation of women is not uncommon, the decision-making bodies and/or
groups remain patriarchal.

Climate Change and Gender

41. Iraq's Central Statistics Administration's data reveal that women are dealing with the
shrinking agricultural land due to drought and desertification. In fact, three million
acres out of the 14 million acres currently available to agriculture face the threat of
desertification. The combined effects have reduced prospects for viable irrigated
agriculture in Iraq. This has led to a reduction in food production and increased competition
for rural communities. It has also negatively impacted the quality of life, especially for women.
Women are more vulnerable than men because they bear the brunt of the
increased burden of fetching water and facilitating other basic household needs from
longer distances as water resources dry up.42 The increasing incidence of drought and
water scarcity is a major challenge faced by women both for food production and in
fetching water for basic household needs.43

There is a paucity of secondary data or studies on the effects of climate change on
women's lives, especially with respect to their role as caregivers and the immediate
effects of climate change. The only document which provides some information on this topic is Iraq's National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Climate Change, submitted to UNFCCC in 2017-2020. It outlines the challenges women face in the Middle East and North
Africa (MENA) region, including Iraq, with respect to climate change in these countries.
A key challenge is that there are limited data available on the effects of climate change,
to understand the strategies adopted by rural women and men to address climate change

impacts on agriculture and rural communities. In the Near East and North Africa region, including Iraq, both women and men work in agriculture with different levels of responsibilities and workloads, which are likely to increase with climate change.

Women's productive and reproductive roles are not sufficiently recognized or accounted for in national policies and programs. This is particularly true in the context of natural disasters influenced or exacerbated by climate change both at the national and regional levels. Women generally face higher risks and more problems due to the impacts of climate change, including water scarcity, food insecurity, and gendered constraints illustrated so far. In a water-scarce country, especially in rural areas, access to water is often limited for women because their mobility and decision-making power are limited. Women are significantly affected by water scarcity due to their role in managing both domestic and productive water use. Their unequal participation in decision-making processes related to water resources management and climate change adaptation often prevent women from contributing fully to climate-related planning, policy-making and implementation.

The strategy highlights the pressing need to identify women's roles and constraints in irrigation and agricultural development and management in the context of climate change. It also emphasizes that gender equality is a key issue for addressing the challenges facing women and men as regards water resource management and climate change adaptation and mitigation for stakeholders, especially the government. This would provide an opportunity to address the barriers and constraints that are currently putting barriers to women's engagement in sustainable water resource management, environmental protection, and climate change adaptation. To overcome these gendered barriers, practitioners must on the hand, succeed in empowering women and mainstreaming gender within water management, agriculture and climate change adaptation policies and programs. This will require a better understanding of suitable capacity, skills and experiences, and limited opportunities and time for working on these issues. The strategy also highlights that there is an urgent need to increase participation in water and agriculture development, and climate change adaptation.

It emphasizes the need for legal and regulatory frameworks as well as institutional arrangements at all levels of governance to ensure women's equal access to water resource management and decision making at different levels: national, local and regional governments. Basin, river, marine and aquifer organizations all need to enable participation in water resource management and climate change adaptation across sectors using gender analysis. An interdisciplinary approach of transboundary water governance is needed for the region. The strategy highlights that this approach has the potential to be truly transformative. It further highlights the need for awareness raising and advocacy to ensure that gender equality is considered as a key issue in the water governance framework. The four main challenges facing women in agriculture and climate change adaptation in Iraq are: 1) women's challenges in water, agriculture and climate change adaptation; 2) women's limited access to land, credit and extension services; 3) gender mainstreaming in water governance and adaptation; and 4) women's participation and empowerment in water governance and climate change adaptation.

45. The findings from community consultations are consistent with secondary data on climate change and gender: that farmers, both women and men, are generally aware of the effects of climate change and recognize its negative impact on agricultural yield, livestock and income. Most of the women and men consulted in the three governorates reported changes in rainfall patterns, water scarcity, soil salinity, higher temperatures, and frequent occurrence of dust storms.

46. With respect to impact of climate change, community consultations indicate that farmers are experiencing difficulties in agricultural activities and livestock farming on account of climate change. The most common problem is a lack of water for irrigation and domestic use as a major challenge due to water shortage (dried up lakes and rivers). A woman from Muthanna also highlighted that climate change has severely affected her livelihood, especially during the summer months of June, July, August (particularly July and August) because of frequent dust storms and high temperatures. Women farmers in Al Anbar reported that they are facing difficulties in feeding their animals due to dry and hot weather. Overall, small-scale farmers’ yield and income has taken a major hit due to effects of climate change, to the extent that many do not depend on agriculture as an occupation anymore. Instead, women have started buying imported vegetables and canned food (powder milk) from the local market, which is comparatively cheaper than what they produce.

47. Another important finding from the consultations is that small-scale farmers are not fully equipped to cope with the effects of climate change to maintain a certain level of agricultural production. This is evidenced by the fact that there has been a significant proportion of farmers leaving the sector. Farmers are using traditional techniques and methods to address effects of climate change, but overall, they are ill-equipped to deal with the adverse impacts of climate change. In response to high temperatures, a prevalent practice is to build a roof over their sheds. Other common coping strategies include planting shade trees around livestock pens or building warehouses to safeguard harvest from storms. This, the challenges stemming from climate change accompanied by rising costs of inputs has rendered agriculture an insufficient livelihood for many farmers in the three governorates.

48. The Climate Change Profile Iraq (2006) acknowledges daunting climate-related challenges faced by small farmers and livestock producers, including decreased rainfall, increased temperatures, soil salinity, and desertification, as well as widespread desertification, but does not specify how women are specifically affected by climate change. The profile also highlights the need for a national strategy to help Iraqis “improve quality of life and livelihood from an environment and health perspective, protect natural environment, and use and support sustainable practices”, and briefly indicates that there is a need to reach out to NGOs and farmers so they can play a role
