In its implementation. However, there is no specific acknowledgment of the role of women in responding to climate change related challenges. The United Nations Development Assistance Framework (UNDAF) 2015-2019**, in partnership with the Government of Iraq, sets out the collective response by the UN system to national development priorities and aligns them with the principles of the UN Development Group (UNDG). The UNDAF prioritizes environmental sustainability, poverty reduction, social inclusion, gender equality and the building of resilience of women, youth and capacity development.

### Key Initiatives in Climate Change & Agriculture with a Gender Focus

49. A GCF readiness support project for Building capacity to Advancing the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process in Iraq, funded by the GCF and implemented by the UNDP Iraq aims to reduce vulnerability to the negative impacts of climate change, especially in developing countries. NAPs are a key tool for national governments to address climate change. NAPs are seen as one of the most important mechanisms for adapting to climate change. The three-year project, funded by the GCF with USD $2.5m, will support Iraq’s NAP process through capacity building and technical assistance in strengthening institutional, technical and financial capacities to ensure that medium- to long-term adaptation strategies are developed and implemented. A key element of this aspect of Iraq's NAP process is to identify, assess and bridge existing gaps in climate knowledge, as these gaps constitute barriers to long-term climate planning across local, national and international levels. The project will also support the NAP process by carrying out to identify the livelihoods and sectors most threatened by climate impacts, as well as their vulnerability.

50. The project launched in September 2020, and aims to mainstream gender in all its activities. It is aiming for 50 percent representation of women in consultations and capacity building workshops. Specific measures for mainstreaming gender are mentioned. The document specifies that vulnerability assessments will focus and report separately on vulnerable groups such as women, youth, indigenous people and persons with disabilities. A special focus on vulnerable groups. The National Adaptation Plan technical team will have greater representation from women and youth. Women’s economic empowerment and representation of civil society organizations representing women, youth and indigenous peoples and women parliamentarians will be ensured. A stock taking exercise to identify the gender gaps in Iraq's NAP process is still being carried out. The draft report and the report on the Climate Risk Assessment is still in progress.

51. Smallholder Agricultural Revitalization Project and the Building Resilience of the Agriculture Sector in a Changing Climate (BRRACC) project in Qadisiyah, Wasit, Misan and Thi-Qar). This project is still in the initial stages of implementation. Under this project, women will be targeted for capacity building on climate-resilient practices and technologies that would enhance crop and livestock production. The project's gender strategy includes the following measures: Gender targets of at least 40 percent for the training of women farmers; a gender-sensitive selection process for other specialists or in have a proven track record of working with women; a tentative pre-selection of women as trainers for the capacity building workshops on climate adaptation (beekeeping, vegetable farming, small livestock rearing, off-farm activities such as sewing, etc.); gender sensitive selection of interventions to maximise returns to women farmers.

**United Nations Country Team Iraq, United Nations Development Assistance Framework 2015-2019, 2014:

### Promoting Sustainable Land Management for Improved Livelihoods in Degraded Areas of Iraq

#### Project Overview
The project aims to reverse land degradation, conserve land and water resources, and improve sustainable livelihoods through a multi-stakeholder approach. It targets national and local actors to promote sustainable land and water management practices, agroforestry systems, and climate resilient agricultural techniques.

### Restoration and Strengthening the Resilience of Agri-Food Systems in Iraq

#### Project Context
The project is aimed at enabling poor smallholder farmers and landless rural households in Baqubah, Misratah, and Tishk-Qar Governorates to improve their livelihoods by enhancing access to agro-ecological resources and water resources. The project will consider and support: (i) the needs, priorities, constraints of women as applicable to horticulture and livestock production; (ii) the role of women in decision-making processes and control over productive resources and agri-food micro-enterprises; and (iii) women's greater vulnerability to climate change.

#### Project Activities
- **Farmer Training:** 75 percent of farmers directly benefit from on-farm water management (OFWM) training, horticultural and livestock equipment, and input packages.
- **Women Empowerment:** 75 percent of women in agri-food enterprises benefiting from post-harvest management (PHM) equipment, utensils, containers, and storage facilities will be trained. The project will undertake gender-sensitive socio-economic baseline surveys, awareness campaigns, and capacity-building activities for women to continue.
- **Gender Analysis:** These analyses will disaggregate data by gender and age to assess smallholder income needs, determine their livelihood skills base, capture their economic contributions, and support input supply and access to credit and agri-business formation. The surveys will further reveal women's challenges and

**Page 147**

54. **Support to Agricultural Livelihoods of Rural and Peri-urban returnees and communities in Ninewah Governorate, Iraq, 2019–2022, FAD** The project focuses on integrated agricultural livelihoods development to increase income, increase resilience and provide nutritious and healthy diets through a comprehensive set of interventions. These include: i) improved access to irrigation water; ii) agricultural inputs, training and strengthening of market linkages – under a scenario of climate change and variable and conflict sensitivity. The main interventions support 1) vulnerable women and youth in peri-urban areas with livelihoods development and marketing systems rehabilitated and strengthened; ii) small-scale agri-food processing, marketing and value addition enterprises developed; iii) improved access to post-harvest processing and marketing systems developed; and iv) smallholder crop and livestock farmers with i) efficient irrigation water use and management measures and technologies (e.g., drip irrigation, sprinkler irrigation, micro-irrigation, drought-tolerant seeds); and vii) improved small-scale animal fodder production, conservation and marketing systems. The project will be specifically targeted for women and youth affected rural and peri-urban areas to participate in and benefit from all sub-programme interventions.

55. **The Project for Sustainable Irrigation Management Using Water Users Associations (WUAs), 2019–2023, JICA** Established in the context of Iraq’s 2017–2021 JICA, the overarching goal of the project was that sustainable water management areas operated by WUAs be expanded to the whole country. The project purpose: sustainable water management model development and expansion through the following three components: 1) Improvement of water management by WUA, following the above-mentioned plan; 2) Improvement of water management by WUA, following the above-mentioned plan; 3) Monitoring and evaluation of the project’s progress. The project aimed to develop the model for improving water management by WUA through formulation and implementation of action plans by WUA in the context of the Water Users Association Development Plan (WADP). Irrigation Development Plan (PID), and necessary manuals were developed in addition to capacity building activities, including training and mentoring. In order to promote gender mainstreaming in the project, gender training was delivered to the majority of all Technical Teams (TT) members from WUAs onsite and 18 WUA members were trained on implementation-oriented activities related to gender activities. In addition, in February 2019, TT and WMT held a briefing session for WUA board members about the project’s progress and provided them with information about the gender activities of the Project. This resulted in the consent of WUA board


---
**Page 148**
---

members to support gender activities under the condition that men and women were separated. In response to prevailing traditional social norm of gender segregation in rural areas, female WMT members were appointed at all WMTs. Lastly, to further extend women's participation to other WUJs, the WUA Management Manual for WMT recommends that WMTs should hold regular meetings with women's representatives as a mechanism to constantly carry out gender activities. As of January 2021, 15 out of 18 WUJs have established women's committees or women's subcommittees. Some WMTCs held women's meetings or established women's subcommittees. The other 8 WMTCs were unable to engage in gender activities due to ongoing restrictions due to the COVID-19 pandemic.

The lesson learnt from this project is (i) gender training can help to create space for women’s inclusion and (ii) indirect representation of women through nominating a small number of women as representatives on WMTs does not constitute meaningful participation of women and completely excludes women-headed households. Therefore, it is recommended that women's committees or women's subcommittees have to have direct representation and for women-headed households to be included in the decision-making body in a culturally sensitive manner with appropriate planning.

56. The Water Management Committee (WMC) project was funded by GCF to building Climate Resilient Livelihoods for Food Insecure People in Southern Iraq. This project aims to introduce practices that build the resilience of vulnerable households whose livelihoods are heavily dependent on agriculture and livestock, through increased irrigation efficiency and water availability; enhanced agricultural productivity through improved access to inputs and services; provision of a gender training for mentoring and capacity strengthening; improved climate decision-support tools and services; as well livelihood diversification through provision of climate-resilient economic activities. The project is implemented by the Iraqi Water Management Project. Gender targets of at least 40 percent for the total of project beneficiaries: working with NGOs in the design phase to ensure that women are included in the project, criteria of working with women; criteria for selection of adaptation and economic diversification activities that is heavily skewed in favor of women's direct participation (vegetable farming, poultry rearing); gender training for WMTs and women's subcommittees to maximize returns to women's adaptive capacities and support their social and economic empowerment. The project has been implemented with a strong implementation team; and adherence to best practice in gender sensitive monitoring and evaluation of programme impact.

57. With regard to the lack of disaggregated data and studies on women’s livelihoods and the impact of climate change on women, stakeholders constituted a challenge in designing the proposal. However, they were able to hold consultations with women and aid organizations to identify the most vulnerable groups and the capacity to conduct the survey. The activities for women included livelihood diversification and creating income generating opportunities through training. Women-headed households were to be targeted through giving them training in farming practices as in the project area they were mostly found to be involved in agriculture.

58. The IOM project was funded by GCF to provide gender-sensitive training for farmers. Women have been included in livelihood activities with typically a 30 percent quota. IOM had trained women farmers through the Directorate of Agriculture for periods ranging from one day to three days, and the training materials used were also being incorporated into the training. The grants given to farmers ranged from USD 1,500 to

Socio-Economic Profile of Target Governorates – Kerbala, Muthanna, and Najaf

### Population:
The latest population model estimated puts total population of the three governorates at about 4 million people, which represents approximately one-third of the total population in the country with target governorates recording slightly higher percentage than the national average. The population density is highest in Najaf among the three governorates and the least populated governorate in Iraq. Among the three governorates, Najaf is the most populated (1.5 million) and Muthanna has the least number of people.

According to a World Bank poverty mapping study (2015), Muthanna is the poorest governorate in Iraq, and proportion of poor traps in Najaf is nearly triple that of Iraq’s national average. The share of people living below the poverty line among the overwhelming majority of households in the three governorates receive Public Distribution System Ration Cards. According to the World Bank, the number of people living in poverty increased by 11.7 percent, making the poverty rate 31.7 percent compared to 20.0 percent prior to the pandemic. The number of poor people in Iraq has risen by almost half of the crisis, adding to already 6.9 million living in poverty before the pandemic.

**62. Children's Health and Nutrition:**
In Kerala, Muthanna, and Nagal, children under five years of age have the highest mortality rate in the range of 30–40 percent, significantly higher than the national average of 28 percent; whereas, the mortality rates in Muthanna and Nagal are relatively lower. The incidence of stunting is also high at all three levels of education. At the lowest level, rates, the incidence of stunting is noticeably greater in Muthanna, almost 14 percent versus 10 percent in Kerala and Nagal (quite high) in comparison to Muthanna and Nagal.


---
**Page 151**

# Women’s Health and Nutrition: Regional data on maternal mortality ratio is unavailable, but the national ratio is around 79 maternal deaths per 100,000 live births.

The proportion of women who have given birth in a health facility varies among the target governorates. Less than one percent of women are severely malnourished in all three governorates, while about 3 to 7 percent of women in Kerbala and Muthanna are moderately or severely underweight. The prevalence of overweight is higher for women in Muthanna compared with Kerbala and Najaf.

The proportion percentage of pregnant women who received care (at least 1 visit by skilled provider) among the three governorates was in Najaf governorate, closely followed by Muthanna and then Kerbala. The highest percentages of women who have undergone at least four ANC visits were 78.4 percent, not only among the three target governorates, but in the country. In addition, the prevalence of women with a skilled provider at birth is higher for these three governorates (around 95 percent or higher), particularly in Najaf (98.5 percent).

### Employment

Muthanna has one of the highest unemployment rates, 14.5 percent, among the three governorates. The unemployment rate is roughly 9.5 percent in Najaf and 7.7 percent in Kerbala. Female unemployment is significantly higher than male unemployment; it is highest in Najaf, 31.4 percent, which is significantly greater than the national average of 22 percent. In comparison to the national level, nearly 90 percent of women are employed in Muthanna and Najaf compared with only about 75 percent in Muthanna, 79 percent in Najaf, and 76 percent in Kerbala; women’s labor force participation is a significant issue in the larger governorates and unemployment is a significant national issue.

### Food Security

The majority of the area in Najaf and Muthanna is categorized as Drought Prone Desert Area, while Kerbala is considered Deficit Food Semi-Arid (DPSA). The percentage of food insecure households in Kerbala and Najaf and comparatively lower than that in Muthanna. The proportion of people vulnerable to food insecurity is alarmingly high in all three governorates—65 percent in Kerbala, 87 percent in Muthanna, and 87 percent in Najaf.

Community consultations suggest that the majority of women in target governorates do not rent land and for agricultural purposes. Women who do own land were reported to have landholding sizes that were considerably smaller than those of men's; 5-10 acres as of wheat versus 450-1,300 acres for men. Similarly, women generally do not rent land (Kerbala: 2 percent; Muthanna: 6 percent; Najaf: 7 percent). In all three governorates, women have five to seven per village, and in fact rent land for farming in their Government ownership.

# Agriculture

In the target governorates, women are involved in the complete farming life cycle, from cultivation to selling produce, i.e., there are few stages or activities exclusive to men. Particularly in Kerbala and Najaf, women play a lead role in the agricultural production process, including sowing (using government-owned tractors in Kerbala), ploughing/irrigation, applying fertilizer, weeding, harvesting, and even marketing. In Najaf, the transplanting of rice seedlings is done exclusively by women. Women are also responsible for managing livestock and poultry in Muthanna; it is generally the women's responsibility to manage family's livestock and poultry in the target governorates. Women are particularly involved in harvesting, growing vegetables and alfalfa. Across all three governorates, women are highly

### 70. The community consultations confirm that women farmers are actively involved in every stage of the farming process from ploughing, sowing/planting seeds, watering, till harvesting and threshing. However, the majority of women interviewed suggested that the type of crops that women farmers cultivate vary by governorate, but nearly all women interviewed reported cultivating vegetables like okra, eggplant, tomatoes, and onions in Kerela. In Muthana, women mainly maintain vegetable gardens, producing vegetables like okra, eggplant, pomegranates, apricots, and oranges. In Najaf, the two main crops women cultivate are wheat and barley.

### 71. Alongside agriculture, livestock rearing and poultry farming is the other major source of income for women in all three governorates. Among the women interviewed from woman-headed households, majority were involved in livestock rearing or poultry farming as a primary activity. In Kerela, two out of five women interviewed reported raising livestock to sell dairy products like milk and cheese, while one raised chickens and geese (known as Al-Basha in the south) to sell in the birds' market. One woman from Muthana also reported selling eggs and poultry meat. However, only one woman, across the three governorates, did not report any other income-generating activity apart from agriculture. In Najaf, where livestock rearing is a primary activity for woman-headed households, as majority of the respondents cited livestock and poultry farming as secondary sources of income for village women, apart from selling vegetables or agricultural produce.

### 72. In community consultations, some women key informants from Najaf and Kerela, but men key informants in the three governorates, did report farm men taking loans for agriculture purposes. However, this was not a common practice among women. The importance of livelihood does not appear to be a common practice across the three governorates.

The only woman farmer who reported taking a loan from the bank had taken it to build a house, not to invest in an income-generating activity.

73. Community consultations suggest that women's role in marketing agricultural produce is limited due to social norms. Women are likely to stay close to their home, farm, or village and sell their produce through informal channels such as markets. Some reported selling their produce through their sons or husbands. However, there are exceptions with some women taking their produce to local markets. Generally, older women tend not to participate in marketing.

Access to Extension Services: With the Ministry of Agriculture (MOA) at the national level, every governorate has a Department/Directorate of Agriculture and Center of Training and Extension. The MOA also has an Agricultural Research Station (ARS) that along with agriculture field units at the village level (cluster of 4-5 villages), which also have a representative from the ARS. These field units are responsible for training, to disseminate latest information on cropping, train and educate farmers, conduct field surveys, and develop yearly plans. In the case of Najaf, there are also three separate units: one in the Agriculture Department, one in the Directorate of Agriculture Al Makhsoo in the south, and one in the center in the Agriculture Directorate. The agricultural extension officers (AEOs) visit farmers' fields to provide training and workshops with farmers in the areas. The themes or topics covered in training or workshops include, but not limited to, tailoring, sewing, raising domestic birds, the importance of preserving water resources, and the use of fertilizers. In addition, there are ongoing girls to study and growing summer vegetables. Table 14 and 15 provide a breakdown of extension staff and agriculture field offices by governorate.

75. Community consultations with men and women across the three governorates, especially women have little to no interaction with government appointed agricultural extension officers in villages, and it is predominantly men who engage with them upon their visit. The reasons for this are that women are not allowed by their families due to social norms and traditions prevent women from interacting with them. In Kerbala, men and women farmers and key informants could not recall extension officers visiting their

**Page 156**

**76.** Women and men farmers offered suggestions on how women could benefit from extension services. Women from Muthanna and Najaf proposed enlisting more women extension officers who would make frequent field visits to train women on modern agricultural practices. In Najaf, women suggested that the training should be conducted in a classroom (if available) or at a community center. They also suggested that the training should deliver seminar designs exclusively for women farmers (existing structures like kiosks in villages can be utilized as training venue) and listen to what women want to learn. Women from Najaf and Muthanna also acknowledged the need to increase number of field visits per year and increasing the frequency of visits by extension officers. In Najaf, it was suggested that field visits should be coordinated with the harvest schedule, while in Najaf it was noted that the production of both vegetables and agricultural inputs (seeds, fertilizers) was important. Some of the women farmers from Karbala also mentioned a significantly greater number of women in the region believe that coordination between village farmers and extension officers is poor. In Muthanna, women suggested that women should receive training on irrigation, marketing, and financial management skills as well as awareness for modern agricultural practices. Women from Najaf and Muthanna also suggested that better coordination between the local government and Department of Agriculture to reach more farmers was also highlighted.

**77.** Women-headed households: Two percent of households in Karbala are women-headed compared with 10 per cent in Najaf. Moreover, women-headed households that own or have control over farmland is comparatively higher in Muthanna (5–8 per cent) and Najaf (30 per cent). In contrast to Karbala, a significantly greater percentage of women-headed households* own livestock in Muthanna (9 to 10 percent) than Karbala and Najaf (0.1 to 5 percent).

women in male-headed households. In all three governorates, these women maintained that they were the primary decision-makers in agriculture/livestock, household expenses, decisions related to children's education, and other household affairs. However, women headed households struggle with finding employment that ensures a stable income for their families. When asked about this issue, they identified the following as key challenges: high costs of inputs, purchase of modern agricultural tools, lack of access to credit facilities, and low prices paid by inadequate. The women acknowledged that the Government of Iraq did offer social welfare salaries to widows and women heads of households, but coverage was reported to be very limited. In addition, many of the women interviewed in Najaf and Muthanna had applied for the stipend, but had still not received it. Some women also mentioned that they used to receive the welfare salary, but recently it had been discontinued.

### 79. Women & Water
In all three governorates, community consultations indicate that water is a critical resource in agriculture/livestock production and multiple purposes, including but not limited to, domestic activities (drinking, laundry, washing), irrigation, and livestock (buffalo/wallow, cleaning animals). Although it is primarily women who collect water for household use, they are not involved with the management of water resources. In addition, women in Muthanna or Najaf live on farms or homes at present, women are not members of any local committee or association that manages water resources.

81. Youth: The prevalence of youth illiteracy and unemployment is highest in Muthanna than Najaf and Kerbala. While the overall youth literacy rate in Kerbala and Najaf is between 75 to 80 percent, it is only about 65 percent in Muthanna. Likewise, the literacy for young women is less than 65 percent in Muthanna; nearly 10 percent less than that of the other two governorates (75.1 to 80 percent). [102]

The youth unemployment rate in Muthanna hovers in the range of 21 to 25 percent, whereas, the rate is somewhere between 11 to 15 percent in Kerbala and Najaf. Interestingly, the youth unemployment rate for young women in Muthanna, 60 percent, than the other two governorates, 36 to 40 percent. Still, Young men’s unemployment rate is significantly higher in Muthanna, 16 to 20 percent, while it is less than 10 percent in Kerbala.

The Youth Development Index (YDI) varies considerably from several socio-economic domains such as political participation/security and freedom/communication, health, education, employment), and as per Iraq Socio-Economic Atlas’s estimates, the YDI index for young men in Najaf is more than 60 percent, while it is less than 50 percent in all three governorates (less than 0.5). Lastly, The proportion of youth who own a mobile phone is more or less the same, 40 to 43 percent, in all three target areas.[12]

### Priorities and Needs of Women and Men Farmers

82. In community consultations, financial assistance form the Government of Iraq was identified as the top most need of both women and men farmers across the three governorates. Financial assistance is provided in various forms such as cash or in the form of provision of modern equipment, machinery, or tools, subsidized inputs, or even loans and grants. Followed by financial support, assistance with marketing was a priority for women farmers in Najaf and Muthanna and men farmers in Kerbala. Some men farmers in Najaf suggested cooperation or partnerships with the government where the farmer and government each receives a pre-determined percentage of the sales, whereas, farmers in Muthanna asked for facilitation with marketing.

124

83. As far as training is concerned, women expressed their need to train on animal rearing and poultry farming, such as provision of high-quality feed/forage for animals and high-quality breeds of animals. It is worth highlighting that a substantial number of women in Kerbala are interested in training on modern agricultural methods and tools application. Finally, women-headed households and key informants also recognized the importance of training and technical assistance in expanding their income-generating activity.

84. Men and women in all three governorates expressed the need for state or institutional support in increasing the availability of water for farming through modern irrigation methods, provision of quality seeds, fertilizers, pesticides/pesticides, financial support for modern agricultural machinery and tools, training, and technical assistance. A vast majority (more than 90%) of both men and women farmers are interested in receiving training and support on crop production, irrigation, water harvesting, and prefer face-to-face training over other methods. Areas of crop production that were most popular among men included irrigation, impregnation technology, and equipment, and kitchen garden are the priority areas for farmers, impacting their income-generating activities.

Interestingly, in all three governorates, a vast majority of men showed interest in receiving training on modern agricultural machinery and tools. A substantial number of women (nearly half or less than 50 percent), which supports the notion that most women are not actively involved in marketing of produce. Furthermore, both women and men generally expressed a need for face-to-face training sessions, with a small proportion seen in Kerbala and women in Najaf also open to alternatives like mass media and mobile applications.

124 See Annex 8A – Report on Community Consultations

125


---
**Page 160**
---

85. Finally, reliable and low-cost electricity could benefit farmers by reducing production costs, particularly through powering irrigation pumps rather than diesel. Regarding the potential benefits for village/household of reliable low-cost electricity, key informants from all three governments recognized the value of electricity to power irrigation or water pumping systems, which would reduce their dependence on diesel generators and related costs. A woman key informant from Najaf also highlighted the usefulness of electricity to store and refrigerate produce, so that consumption can be spread between harvest seasons.

Community Feedback on Climate Wise Women and Women in Water Use Associations

Climate Wise Women:

86. Despite minimal prior engagement with NGOs and social programmes, women farmers are interested in seeking advice and training from a Climate Wise Woman (CWW) in their village, and many would also be interested in taking up the role. All the women farmers interviewed were aware of the CWW concept and expressed willingness to seek advice from such an individual. Majority of the women farmers who were interested in being trained by a CWW were from Najaf (85 percent), Kerbala (86 percent), followed by Najaf (73 percent), and then Muthanna (62 percent). Women who were not interested in becoming a CWW cited local customs and traditional practices as reasons. Some women also mentioned that they would be unable to attend training sessions due to their commitments at home or other commitments at home as reason. The interest in taking up the role of CWWS was considered to be higher among women with children, as they had more time than others for any schedules and responsibilities. A woman key informant from Muthanna pointed out that holding training sessions during summer would be a more suitable time for women to participate.

With respect to selections for the selection criteria of a CWW, while respondents were not clear about the age (ranging from students to middle-age), there was a strong emphasis on relevant qualifications, including formal education and agricultural experience. The most common qualification among the three regions. The FGDs' respondents in Muthanna suggested titles of "Trainer" for the CWWS in Najaf, considered as an "Agricultural Advisor", and the most suggested title in Kerbala was "Climate Wise Woman".

Women's participation in Water User's Associations

87. Community consultations suggest that at present, majority of the women in the target group are not members of any water user organizations or associations, mainly because of absence of such organizations or projects, as well as local traditions and customs. Based on interviews with women-headed households and key informants from all three regions, there were no women's groups or associations operating in their respective villages, other than the Water Users Associations (WUA) in Muthanna and Najaf. The WUAs are not gender-sensitive organizations, and neither NGOs and the WUA does not provide support services. Key informants were not aware of any NGOs or support organizations that were engaging local farmers (women and men) in the surveyed areas. Two key informants (person) from Najaf did recall a


---
**Page 161**
---

livelihoods project delivered by IOM three years ago, but, at present, there were no on-going projects they were aware of.

88. Most women in Kerbala and Najaf would like women to be part of a committee on water allocation, while an overwhelming majority of men and women in Muthanna believe that decisions regarding water distribution are men's responsibility. In both FGDs, all male and female respondents in Muthanna, and nearly half of female respondents in Kerbala, were in agreement that women should not join public committees, such as water allocation committees. However, in Najaf, 50% of the male respondents felt that decisions regarding water distribution is men's responsibility. Conversely, other female respondents felt that women should be part of a committee on water allocation and/or giving women membership in groups and associations that decide water allocation. Respondents noted that women play a prominent role in on-farm and off-farm activities and therefore their participation would have an important developmental component in each one.

While about half of the men in Najaf also concurred with women's representation in WUA, most female respondents recognized the value of women's participation in such groups. Women in Najaf highlighted that as women are knowledgeable about local geography, water sources, and irrigation systems, their insights on matters related to irrigation and livestock farming, their insights on the matter of water distribution are crucial. Moreover, some women respondents also pointed out that as women account for 50% of the community, it would make sense to have representation in public forums.

# Fund-level impacts

* A1: Increased resilience through increased water-energy access and power generation
    * A1.0 Increased resilience of communities and regions
    * A1.1 Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security
* A2: Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions
    * A2.0 Increased resilience of communities and regions
    * A2.1 Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security

# Project-level outcomes

## Outcome 1: Increased water availability for irrigation farmers through improved water conveyance efficiency by investing in a closed irrigation system and promoting the adoption of renewable energy (A1) (A2)

* D1: Increased adoption of practices and technologies to address climate risks for men and women farmers through training to increase water use efficiency and promote the adoption of renewable energy
    * A1.0 Increased resilience of communities and regions
    * A1.1 Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security

## Outcome 2: Improved policy environment for efficient water and energy management enabled through incorporation of climate change adaptation into key national water and energy policies (A3)

* D2: Policy environment for efficient water and energy management enabled through incorporation of climate change adaptation into key national water and energy policies
    * A1.0 Increased resilience of communities and regions
    * A1.1 Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security
